5. **The finite matrix is an executable artifact, not a checklist.** `BlockModeMatrixTest` (pure JVM) carries the state table in its KDoc and pins every cell: A, W, Q alone; W+Q ("05:00–12:00 AND 2h a day" — both apply independently, sharing one day scope); A+W / A+Q / A+W+Q ≡ A (inert members must not restrict an active day further); "none" is unreachable from the editor and inert if a legacy row carries it; expiry lifts every mechanism at once and lifts W-alone and Q-alone individually; a paused rule is dropped by `RulesSnapshot.build` before any decision (pinned for always-on and for W/Q); the day boundary is the rule's-zone midnight (a Saturday rule lifts at Sunday 00:00); the day is judged in the rule's zone, not the caller's (one instant is Saturday in UTC and still Friday in UTC−11); and `BlockDecisionEngine.blocksNow` (the row's claim) matches enforcement for day-scoped mechanisms. The engine's shape is what lets the axes compose without per-cell tests: expiry short-circuits first, then one `active` boolean gates all three mechanisms (BlockDecisionEngine.kt `evaluateRule`), so each axis is pinned against every mechanism by the tests above rather than by re-deriving the cross product. `RuleModeTest` pins the form's pure logic (parsing, validation, composition), `ScheduleSummaryTest` the row's claims per combination, and `UsageAccountingEngineTest` gained the day-gate legs (inactive day: no accrual, no block, no row; stale row still rolls; active day accrues and blocks).

**Verification:** JVM **768 tests, 0 failures, 0 errors, 0 skipped** across 102 classes (40 new: BlockModeMatrixTest 18, RuleModeTest 9, ScheduleSummaryTest 5, UsageAccountingEngineTest +3, plus formatter/parse legs). `:app:assembleDebug` green. Emulator-5554 (API 34) walks, all via the debug harness + real UI: (D1) quota-only rule (no window at all), usage seeded at exactly the 2-minute limit → `MyExodusUsage: observe … 120000ms/120000ms Block` and the wall rendered "Chrome is done for today — Today's time limit is used up. It resets tomorrow." (screencap); (D2) same seed with days = all-except-today → `decide … Allow`, Chrome stayed in the foreground, **no `observe` line at all**, tracker row unchanged at 120000 ms (the day gate, not the quota, refused); (D3) same seed with days = today-only → `observe +0ms → 120000ms/120000ms Block`; (D4) editor walk — a quota-only rule typed as Hours 8 / Minutes 0 showed `= blocked after 8h a day`, saved, and the row read "8h daily limit · weekends"; (D5) raising that limit mid-day 2m → 8h unblocked the app while accrual continued under the new limit (row 120460 → 160591 ms across the foreground span); (D6) always-on + weekends saved through the same form → row "Always on · weekends / Blocked now" and the wall "Chrome is blocked"; (D7) days flipped to weekdays → row "Always on · weekdays / Not active now" and `decide … Allow` with Chrome foreground; (D8) **window mechanism, day-active polarity** — a schedule-only rule (11:00 AM–7:00 AM overnight form, weekends, today = Sunday) saved through the same editor → row "11:00 AM–7:00 AM (overnight) · weekends / Blocked now", `MyExodusBlocker: decide … -> Block`, and the wall rendered "Chrome is blocked / Blocked by your MyExodus rules" (screencap); (D9) **window mechanism, day-inactive polarity** — the same rule's days flipped to weekdays → row "… · weekdays / Not active now", `decide … -> Allow`, Chrome foreground, no wall. The seeded rule was deleted through the UI at the end (which also rendered the empty state).

**Trade-off Accepted:** A legacy rule that stores always-on + a limit keeps its stored value (the engine treats it as always-on; the editor zeroes both on the next save). Normalizing at read time was rejected: it would rewrite user data without a user action, and the matrix pins the behavior instead. `blocksNow` reports **no claim** (`null` label) for any quota rule, because the row has no usage feed — "Ask, don't guess" beats a pill that could contradict the engine.

**Consequences:** The three mechanisms are now independently expressible, so the user's reported trap (a limit silently overridden by an always-on default) cannot recur: an unselected switch stores zero. Day scope is one rule-level field, so no two mechanisms can disagree about which days the rule runs — and both the row and the engine read the same `activeDaysOfWeek`. New pattern entry: `app/docs/engineering-patterns-reference.md` §2, "One scope control governs every mechanism in the object it scopes"; §1, "the finite-state matrix as an executable spec". The emulator walk now covers all three mechanisms and both day-scope polarities per mechanism (quota D1–D3, always-on D6–D7, window D8–D9), so the earlier residual gap (window's day-scope leg being JVM-only) is closed; the two mechanisms that remain device-identical are combinations that produce no distinct user-visible behavior (W+Q inside a window blocks via the window first — the engine checks `withinWindow` before the quota — and outside a window W+Q is Q-alone, which D1 walks).

---

**ADR \#69 Title: The Form's Input Domain Is Part of the Scenario Space (2026-09-20) — a 30-Hour Daily Limit Saved, and a Rejected Save the User Could Not See**

**Status:** Accepted; implemented + JVM-verified + emulator-verified 2026-09-20. Four defects fixed; the one semantic fork the original draft left "open for a ruling" (overnight day ownership) was ruled by the user the same day and is Decision 5 below — code changed, tests re-pinned, device-walked.

**Context:** ADR #68 pinned the *mechanism* matrix but treated the form as a given. The user then typed "blocked for 30 hrs a day", the form accepted it, and the rule could never fire — a usage day is 24 h long, so a limit at or above one day is unreachable by construction. His framing: "i just typed blocked for 30 hrs a day and it was accepted hence why i said run through as many scenarios as possible" — i.e. the finite space includes the **input domain**, not just the option combinations. An audit for the same class (state the UI accepts, engine can never act on) found three more, plus a fourth found while device-walking the fixes.

**Decision:**

1. **A daily limit must be under 24 hours** (`ModeError.LimitTooLarge`). `validateBlockMode` rejects `limitTotalMinutes >= MINUTES_PER_DAY`; the bound is on the **total**, so "0 h 1440 m" is rejected like "30 h 0 m", and `parseLimitTotalMinutes("30","0")` is caught at the same gate. Inert beside always-on, like every other limit check. The field-level preview turns into the error text (`editor_daily_limit_too_large`) in the error color, so the reason sits next to the input that caused it. 23 h 59 m — the largest limit that can still fire — is accepted (the boundary is pinned from both sides).

2. **An expiry in the past is no longer offerable** (`NotPastDates : SelectableDates` on the date picker). The engine's Rule 3 lifts an expired rule before any mechanism runs, so a past date was a save that silently produced a dead rule. Whole-day comparison at UTC epoch-day granularity, spanning the whole picker's navigation range rather than one month.

3. **The last remaining day cannot be cleared** — the guard moved out of the chip's `onClick` into the pure `toggleDay`. A days set with no day never matches, so the rule could never fire; the guard now protects both editors (rules + recovery risk windows) and is testable without Compose. A short string is padded to seven characters before flipping, so a truncated value can never silently become "no day matches".

4. **A rejected save scrolls its message into view** (found while device-walking #1). The `ErrorBanner` renders above the form while Save renders below it; from the bottom of a long form the banner appeared off-screen, so tapping Save looked like it did nothing. `LaunchedEffect(error) { animateScrollTo(0) }` brings the message to the user rather than waiting for them to find it.

**Verification (input-domain batch; the ruling later the same day re-ran the suite — 772, see its verification below):** JVM **771 tests, 0 failures, 0 errors, 0 skipped** across 102 classes (`RuleModeTest` +2, `RuleTargetsTest` +1 pinning the four input-domain rules and the composition invariants). `:app:assembleDebug` green. Emulator-5554 (API 34) walks, all through the real UI: (E1) Hours 30 / Minutes 30 → inline error "A daily limit must be under 24 hours…" under the fields, Save rejected, and the Rules list still read "No blocks yet" afterwards (screencap); (E2) no mechanism selected → "Choose at least one way to block." banner, Save rejected; (E3) the same rejection from the **bottom** of the form now scrolls the banner into view (the #4 fix); (E4) the boundary — 23 h 59 m showed `= blocked after 23h 59m a day`, saved, and the row read "23h 59m daily limit"; (E5) window start == end → "Start and end time can't be the same. Pick a different end time." (banner scrolled into view) with a valid limit still enabled beside it, proving the check is independent of the other switches; (E6) the day guard — Weekends → turn Sat off → only Sun remains → tapping Sun leaves it selected (no reachable "no days" rule); (E7) the date picker greys out every day before today and accepts the 25th, with the Ends row reading back "Sep 25, 2026" (the stored → picker → stored round trip). The test rule was deleted through the UI at the end.

**Trade-off Accepted:** A date is pre-selected when the picker opens from "Never", so tapping OK without choosing sets an expiry the user did not pick. Left as-is: OK means "accept the shown date", and the Ends row immediately shows what was stored. Making OK require an explicit tap was rejected as it would need a state the picker does not expose.

**5. The start day owns an overnight window's after-midnight hours (user ruling 2026-09-20, on the fork this ADR's first draft left open).** The draft recorded the two readings without picking one: `BlockDecisionEngine` gated the window on the **current** day in the rule's zone (a Friday-night rule stopping at Saturday 00:00), while the recovery plan's `RiskWindowEvaluator` gave the after-midnight hours to the day the window **starts** (`now.toLocalDate().minusDays(1)`) and documented that as intended. Ruling: start-day wins, "matches how people describe Friday night". `windowDayActive` now judges the window's day leg as: overnight form (`endMin < startMin`) and the clock is before the window's end → the **previous** day in the rule's zone; otherwise the current day. A Friday 22:00–07:00 rule therefore still blocks Saturday 03:00 and lifts at 07:00 — same as the risk-window evaluator. The always-on and quota gates stay on the **current** day (`dayActive` unchanged): a quota is metered per calendar day, so its day scope is the day the usage is counted on, and `dayScopeCoversToday` gates exactly that path (ADR #68). The editor's copy ("Overnight — ends the next day.") needed no change — it describes the wall-clock shape, which was never the ambiguity.

**Verification (ruling):** JVM — `BlockModeMatrixTest` 19 tests (was 18): the overnight cell rewritten to pin start-day ownership (weekend-scoped: `sat(23)` / `sun(3)` / `mon(3)` all Block, `mon(7)` lifts) plus `windowOnly_overnightForm_startDayOutOfScope_doesNotSpill`, the discriminator the current-day reading fails — a Friday-only rule blocks `sat(3)`. `RiskWindowEvaluatorTest` (7) and `BlockingPipelineTest` (15, every-day-scoped overnight rule) unchanged and green — the other implementation the ruling aligned with, and the neighbor that had nothing to change. Full suite: **772 tests, 0 failures, 0 errors, 0 skipped** across 102 classes. Device (emulator-5554, Sunday 12:41 EAT — inside the post-midnight leg of a 22:00–15:00 window, so the two readings predict opposite outcomes and no clock change is needed): (E8) window-only rule (Chrome), 10:00 PM–3:00 PM, days = **Saturday only** → row read "10:00 PM–3:00 PM (overnight) · Sat / **Blocked now**", Chrome launched → `MyExodusBlocker: decide com.android.chrome gen=4 -> Block` and the wall rendered "Chrome is blocked" (screencap); (E9) the same rule's days flipped to **Sunday only** → "… · Sun / **Not active now**", Chrome launched → `decide … -> Allow` and Chrome stayed in the foreground. A current-day reading predicts the exact inverse of both legs (Sat-only would allow, Sun-only would block). The test rule was deleted through the UI at the end; the accessibility service switched on for the enforcement log was switched back off.

**Consequences:** Rule semantics now have one definition each: the overnight window means the same thing on the enforcement path, the rule row, and the recovery risk-window evaluator, so no user-visible claim can disagree with the engine about a wall-clock window (new pattern entry: `app/docs/engineering-patterns-reference.md` §1, "a rule concept must mean one thing, in every engine that evaluates it"). The quota and always-on gates deliberately keep the current-day reading — a quota is metered per calendar day, so its day scope is the day the usage is counted on (`dayScopeCoversToday`, ADR #68). Every editor-reachable rule can now fire or expire as the row claims. New pattern entry: `app/docs/engineering-patterns-reference.md` §1, "the input domain is part of the finite-state space" — the ADR #68 matrix pinned *which combinations exist* and missed *which values are impossible*, which is exactly how a 30-hour limit got through a matrix that looked complete. The `toggleDay` guard living in the pure function (not the click handler) is the same lesson §1 already carries: a rule that can never fire should be unrepresentable, and the place to make it unrepresentable is the shared function both editors route through.

**ADR \#70 Title: The Metering Baseline Is Dual-Stack, and the Plan's IPv4-Only Contract Item Is Inapplicable (Domain Usage Metering S0, 2026-09-20) — Two Baseline Findings Ruled by the User, Plus the S1 Static-Check Substitution**

**Status:** Accepted; both rulings made by the user 2026-09-20 before any feature code (plan §8 S0.2 STOP list). S1 static checks implemented and JVM-verified the same day.

**Context:** The domain-usage-metering plan (`app/plans/myexodus-domain-usage-metering-implementation-plan (1).md`) gates S1+ on a Baseline Contract P1–P10 verified against the shipped `DnsSinkholeVpnService`, with a STOP list of P1/P3/P4/P5/P6/P7. The verification (`app/docs/domainusage-baseline-check.md`) found two literal deviations from the plan's assumed baseline — both *beneficial* compared to what the plan described, which is exactly the case the plan has no procedure for: a FAIL that is not a defect.

**Decision:**

1. **The TUN stays dual-stack; plan D-15's "The TUN itself stays IPv4-only" is recorded as inapplicable to this baseline (P6).** The shipped builder adds `fd00:14:14::1/128` and a `::/0` route (ADR #19, `ipv6_sinkhole` one of the seven spike gates passed 3× on-device). Stripping it to satisfy the plan's sentence would reroute device IPv6 traffic *outside* the TUN — a much larger unmetered hole than the AAAA suppression the IPv4-only assumption was meant to avoid, and contrary to the delivered architecture. Consequences accepted: FR-8's shaping table is implemented unchanged (its cost stays narrow — host-specific, rule-enabled only); IPv6 flows are in the packet path and attributable by the same SNI/DNS-fallback logic, so I-13's literal-IPv6 check passes by attribution rather than by AAAA suppression; §8 S8.3's "do not add IPv6 addresses or routes" now reads as *add nothing new* — the existing configuration is untouched.

2. **The Baseline UDP relay's destination-keyed flow table is extended to the full 5-tuple when the UDP tap is wired (P3, at S6).** The shipped key is `"<dstIp>(<version>):<dstPort>"` with a single-requester reply path, so two apps talking to the same `server:port` share one relay socket and the second app's replies are misrouted — a pre-existing baseline limitation, not a metering defect. The plan's UDP design assumes true per-flow keying (`UdpAdmission` per 5-tuple, §4.2/§6.4), and §3.3 sanctions modifying the Baseline UDP relay; the extension fixes the misrouting at the same time. Ruled to happen at S6 rather than S0 because it is a behavioral change to a device-proven component and belongs with the wiring that depends on it.

3. **The S1.4 wall-clock CI check uses a filesystem `grep` where the plan's literal command uses `git grep`.** `git grep` searches tracked files only, so a violation in a not-yet-committed `domainusage` file would pass the check that exists to catch it. Same paths (`*/domainusage/*`), same pattern, same "matches only `SystemWallClock.kt`" rule; the scan root is overridable so the check can be tested against a planted violation (U-13). Recorded here because it is a deviation from a literal plan instruction, not a detail the plan left open.

**Trade-off Accepted:** Keeping the dual-stack TUN means the plan's stated *benefit* of an IPv4-only TUN ("IPv6 traffic is not visible") does not apply — IPv6 metering correctness now rests on the same attribution logic as IPv4 rather than on the traffic being absent. Accepted: the same code path is the one the plan already specifies, and the alternative (rerouting IPv6 outside the TUN, or silently stripping a device-proven component to satisfy a sentence) is strictly worse.

**Consequences:** The first ruling's dual-stack consequence reached S8 concretely: §6.10's UDP-drop ICMP Port-Unreachable is implemented for **both** families (RFC 4443 type 1 code 4 for v6 with the pseudo-header checksum, alongside the plan's RFC 792 v4 form) — an IPv4-only nudge would leave exhausted-rule IPv6 datagrams silently dropped, defeating the nudge's own fast-fallback purpose on half the traffic (`domainusage-implementation-log.md`, S8 deviation 1). The plan's §16 item 5 NAT64 reasoning still holds as a *cost* of the AAAA-NODATA measure (host-specific, rule-enabled only) but its IPv4-only *benefit* claim is void here. Any feature code that assumes IPv4-only inputs must use the existing 16-byte/`isV6` forms instead — the plan's own 16-byte `DnsObservationStore` keys already match. The S0 gate closed with these two rulings recorded, and S1 (package, clocks, mode flag, CI checks, PSL pinning) landed against them: `app/docs/domainusage-implementation-log.md` carries the per-step record, including the S1.2 `WallClock.today(zone)` signature — the plan's `today()` no-arg wording would re-read `ZoneId.systemDefault()` per call and so break §4.8's anchored-zone rule for the sake of a signature shape.

**ADR \#71 Title: A Limit-Governed Website Rule Leaves the Policy Blocklist — the Usage Shaper Owns Its DNS Answer (Domain Usage Metering S10 finding, 2026-09-21)**

**Status:** Accepted; fix + JVM tests landed, device-verified on the X6532 the same day.

**Context:** The §12.2 matrix smoke run found the feature's core promise unreachable for its own rules. The plan's §1 states: "Traffic to that site and its subdomains consumes the allowance while data is flowing. **At the limit** the site stops loading until the next local day," and FR-7 makes NXDOMAIN the *exhaustion* signal (`total ≥ dailyLimitMs`). But `DnsSinkholeVpnService.handleDns` consults `DomainBlocklist` **first** (the S0-hazard-1 composition: one authority per query), and `DomainBlocklist.fromUrlPatterns` admitted every host-level website rule — including the limit-governed ones. A rule whose only mechanism is a daily limit was therefore NXDOMAIN'd from the first query of the day: the site could never load long enough to accrue a byte, the shaper never saw the query, and the Rules screen would read "Used today: 0 / 30 min" forever under a site that simply did not load. Device evidence chain (raw DNS probes from the device shell): the ruled name `du-a.192.0.2.99.nip.io` answered `rcode=3 an=0 ns=0` — the baseline builder's SOA-less NXDOMAIN — with `dns_nxdomain_served=0`, proving the engine never answered it; a genuinely nonexistent name answered `rcode=3 an=0 ns=1` (upstream SOA) and `example.com` `rcode=0 an=2`, separating blocklist-synthesized from upstream answers. `BlockDecisionEngine.evaluateRule` (the accessibility path) already honoured `dailyLimitMs`, `isAlwaysOn` and the window, so the defect was isolated to the DNS blocklist.

**Decision:**

1. **`DomainBlocklist.fromUrlPatterns` admits a rule only when `isAlwaysOn || dailyLimitMs <= 0`.** A rule whose block is governed solely by its daily limit is *metered*, not blocked: `DomainUsageEngine`/`DnsShaper` owns its DNS answer — forwarded while under the limit (NODATA for AAAA/HTTPS/SVCB only, the D-14 SNI-visibility measure) and NXDOMAIN once exhausted (FR-7/FR-8). The predicate is deliberately *not* "has a daily limit": an always-on rule with a limit must keep blocking unconditionally — excluding it would hand its A queries to the shaper, which answers NODATA (not NXDOMAIN) for a not-yet-exhausted rule, i.e. an always-on site would start resolving. A limit-plus-window rule is excluded too: under the editor's OR-of-mechanisms semantics the window is the Accessibility engine's to enforce (it has the clock), and keeping it in the blocklist made its limit meter nothing, exactly like the limit-only case.
2. **The window-only ceiling stands, now explicit.** A rule whose only mechanism is a time window still blocks all day at the DNS layer (the port has no clock); the shaper does not see window-only rules at all (the provider emits only limits). Unchanged pre-existing behavior, kept because removing such a rule from the blocklist would *unblock it entirely* at resolution time rather than enforce the window.

**Evidence (post-fix, same device, same rule):** A query → `rcode=0 an=1 rdata=192.0.2.99` (resolves while under the limit); AAAA → `rcode=0 an=0 ns=1` (the engine's SOA-carrying NODATA) with `dns_nodata_aaaa` incremented. JVM: `DomainBlocklistTest` +4 — limit-only excluded, limit-plus-window excluded, always-on-plus-limit still blocked, window-only still blocked (the ceiling pinned as a test, not a comment).

**Trade-off Accepted:** For a limit-plus-window rule, the DNS layer no longer blocks during the window (it cannot evaluate windows at all); window enforcement is the Accessibility engine's, which is the mechanism that has a clock and already enforced it. Accepted: the alternative — keeping such rules blocked all day — silently voids their daily limit, the exact failure this ADR fixes, and window-only rules keep their V1 all-day network block.

**Consequences:** The S0 "one authority per query" composition is intact (blocklist first, then shaper); what changed is *which* rules the blocklist holds. `DomainBlocklist`'s class doc now carries both the metered-rule exclusion and the surviving window ceiling. The §12.2 matrix was re-run on the patched build, and the fix unblocked the legs whose rules are limit-governed.

**ADR \#72 Title: The DNS Observation Store Is Family-Symmetric — AAAA Answers Are Recorded, Closing a Silent Fail-Open for Every IPv6 Flow (Domain Usage Metering S10 finding, 2026-09-21)**

**Status:** Accepted; fix + JVM tests landed, device-verified on the X6532 the same day.

**Context:** §6.4 attributes every non-443 TCP flow, and every UDP flow, at open and *only* from the DNS observation store (`AttributionResolver.byDns`). `DnsShaper.onResponse` recorded just the A branch — `record.type == TYPE_A && rdata.size == 4` — so a flow to an IPv6 destination could never match a candidate: `candidates(ip)` returned empty, `byDns` returned `Dns.None`, and the flow fell through as unattributed. Nothing counted it, nothing metered it, nothing ever reset it. The store itself had been 16-byte-ready from S3 (`keyOf` keys 16 raw bytes, maps 4-byte IPv4 into `::ffff:0:0/96`), and §6.4's design attributes v6 flows the same way it attributes v4 ones — the recorder was the only half of the pair that assumed v4, which is exactly the asymmetry ADR #70 (dual-stack TUN) had already ruled against.

The defect is invisible on an IPv4-only client: every flow that matters has an A answer. It becomes a **silent fail-open on any IPv6-carrying path** — a v6-only carrier, an IPv6-preferring app, or a literal-address connection to a site whose AAAA is the only answer. Both metering and enforcement are lost, and no counter moves to say so.

**Evidence (device, X6532, §12.2 I-13):** pre-fix the scenario's whole v6 leg read as no-ops — the literal connection to the ruled host completed (server-side `accept port=9443 addr=v6`), `rst_injected` stayed at its stale value, `flows_attributed_total` did not move, and the rule's usage stayed `ms=0` for the full 65-second streaming leg, so the rule could not even reach exhaustion. Post-fix, same device and same script: `flows_attributed_total` and `dns_fallback_attributed` both increment on the v6 literal, the 65-second stream accrues to exhaustion, and the literal connection is reset (`rst=1→2`). JVM: `DnsShaperTest` +2 — `aaaaRecordsAreRecordedSoV6FlowsCanBeAttributed` (the attribution the fix exists for, with the reason in the test body) and `anAaaaRecordWhoseRdataIsNotSixteenBytesIsNeverRecorded` (the size guard the A branch already had). 1014 tests, 0 failures.

**Decision:** `onResponse` walks AAAA records exactly as it walks A records: the same alias-chain owner check, the same `rdata.size == 16` guard, the same TTL handoff. No other change — TTL, cap, eviction and expiry all live in the store and were already family-agnostic.

**Trade-off Accepted:** A v6 answer now occupies an observation slot it previously left empty, so the store fills faster on dual-stack browsing. Accepted without a bound change: `DNS_OBS_MAX_IPS` is an access-ordered LRU and the per-IP name list is capped at `DNS_OBS_MAX_NAMES_PER_IP`, so the worst case is bounded eviction of *stale* entries that the v6 flow would otherwise have made useless anyway — a v4-mapped key and its v6 counterpart are deliberately distinct entries (a v4-only and a v6-only host are different endpoints).

**Consequences:** §6.4's DNS-fallback path is now family-symmetric, which is the property ADR #70's dual-stack ruling requires of it: any flow the TUN carries is attributable, and a network that speaks only IPv6 gets metering and enforcement rather than a silent pass. I-13's "connection fails for that host specifically" is reachable again, and I-13b (an unrelated v6 host still resolving and still reachable) passes alongside it.

---

**ADR \#73 Title: The Enforcement Stack Is One Process, So Accounting Gaps Are Repaired From the Platform's Own Record (Daily-limit diagnosis, 2026-09-22)**

**Status:** Accepted; D1/D3 built, JVM-tested (12 new tests) and device-verified on the X6532 the same day. D2 (a local accounting journal) remains unbuilt — see the plan's §8.

**Context:** The owner reported a 2-hour Instagram limit that did not hold on 2026-09-21 — the app stayed usable against Digital Wellbeing's record. A full measurement campaign on 2026-09-22 found **no** counting defect: continuous dwell 0.994×, a live 15-minute chain consuming its 900 s in ~905 s with `blocked=true` at 904.8 s, both warnings delivered within one tick of target (324.7 s / 624.7 s against 300/600), and a ten-hop app-hopping pattern at 1.042× — over-counting, the opposite of the symptom. Planning v2 §15 row 4 mitigated OEM kills with *"Path A remains authoritative when available"*, which presumes Path A and Path B are separable failure domains. They are not: `grep android:process` over every manifest returns nothing, so the accessibility service, the foreground service and the VPN share one ProcessRecord and one kill takes all three together. Decision 17's reactive battery-exemption prompt — the mitigation for exactly that kill — was never built: no `ACTION_REQUEST_IGNORE_BATTERY_OPTIMIZATIONS` exists anywhere in the tree, `ProtectionChecks.batteryExempt` only reads and displays the state, and the X6532's doze whitelist was empty. Planning v2 §12 had already recorded the cost of that state on this hardware: *"XOS kills the FGS-hosting process as `cached idle & background restricted` and the START_STICKY restart was not prompt (~17 min…) with battery optimization not exempt."* Every fast (~1 s) restart measured the same day was a charger artifact. In such a window nothing accrues **and** nothing can block — the one failure mode that leaves the counter below the platform's record while the limit silently fails to hold.

**Evidence (device, X6532, 2026-09-22):** The repair leg held the process down with `am force-stop` (with the exemption granted, the sticky restart is ~1 s — under the repair floor — so no natural gap was reachable). Bolt foregrounded, app force-stopped, 183 s of tracked-app use with the app dead, relaunched: `gap repair: credited 182853ms to ee.mtakso.client over a 183059ms unobserved window`, and the stored counter moved `1339746 → 1522599` ms — the dead window absorbed to within 0.1% of the platform's own record. Notably, `am force-stop` also drops the app's accessibility binding until re-bound; the existing degraded surfaces already report that state.

**Decision:** (1) **The gap is bounded by the platform's exit record, not by a heartbeat.** `ActivityManager.getHistoricalProcessExitReasons` already provides the death timestamp, and the existing §8.2 watermark (`lastKnownGoodExitCheckEpochMillis`) already bounds which exits are new — so the unobserved span is known exactly, at no new write cost. (2) **Credit comes from the platform's own foreground record.** `UsageStatsManager.queryEvents` supplies `ACTIVITY_RESUMED`/`ACTIVITY_PAUSED` marks; the repair sums one tracked package's foreground time, clipped to the window. (3) **The window ends at process start, never at repair time** (`System.currentTimeMillis() - (elapsedRealtime - android.os.Process.getStartElapsedRealtime())`), so it can never overlap a span a live path already credited — which is what makes double-crediting structurally impossible rather than merely unlikely. (4) **Bounded deliberately:** floor 20 s (below it, ordinary restart churn risks double-crediting a normal close-tick tail), cap 6 h (beyond it, assume a stale record), clipped to local midnight so credit lands on today's row. *(Revision 2026-09-28, ADR #103: the cap is **removed** and the window start moved from the death timestamp to the newest journal observation — the plan text this ADR implements, v3 §5 D3, says "No cap. A cap would re-introduce under-counting… The floor and the clip are the only bounds", and its span is `(last_credited_wall_ms, now]`. The 6 h cap refused exactly the long OEM-freezer windows this mechanism exists to repair; see #103 for the device evidence.)* (5) **One writer.** The repair submits through the same `processUsageObservation` transaction every other credit uses, so thresholds, warnings and the block verdict still evaluate once, in order. (6) **The binder half rides the isolated tick pool.** `queryEvents` is the exact call class the 2026-08-30 finding named as able to wedge indefinitely on this device, and the poll loop already isolates every such call for that reason; the repair's exit-record read and event query go through the same pool under the same hard bound, so a wedge abandons a thread and skips the repair rather than stalling anything.

**Trade-off Accepted:** NFR3 ("duration math uses only `elapsedRealtime` deltas within a single boot session") is amended by a narrow carve-out: the repair derives duration from `UsageStatsManager` timestamps, once per process start, over a span this process demonstrably did not observe. Decision 4 is untouched — it forbids inferring duration from *"elapsed time since the last event"*, and the repair infers nothing: it reads the platform's explicit resumed/paused pairs. Planning v2 §16 Q3 already contemplated `queryEvents` for live accounting on this device, with the measured caveat that sojourns under ~1.5–2 s can be missed — an under-count in the repair, logged, and preferable to a silent zero.

**Consequences:** §15 row 4's mitigation is replaced by a mechanism that holds regardless of topology: a kill now costs at most the repair floor (20 s) rather than the whole outage. The counter can no longer sit below Android's own record after a death, so "the limit didn't hold and the number looked low" has one fewer explanation. What is **not** fixed: the outage itself (the app is still dead during it — blocks still cannot run), a gap inside a single boot session with no exit record, and post-hoc diagnosis of a bad day, which still self-destructs under Decision 20's rollover-in-place — that last one is D2, unbuilt. The battery exemption remains the only mitigation for the outage itself, and it is granted on the owner's device but not prompted for by any code path beyond the existing Settings row.

---

**ADR \#74 Title: The Bottom Navigation Bar Is a Floating Notched Slab — Desert Dawn Port of the Reference Study, and Six Always-On Labels Supersede ADR #47's Selected-Only Consequence (2026-09-22)**

**Status:** Accepted; user-approved 2026-09-22 (design chosen from the supplied reference folder, two sign-off questions answered), built and device-verified on the X6532 the same day in both themes at both extreme tabs.

**Context:** The owner asked for the bottom navigation to be redesigned and supplied `/Users/musila/Documents/ReferenceInspirationUI` as the source of truth for the look (the online-research step was waived for this task only). The chosen study is a floating rounded slab with a circular bite cut out of its top edge around the active item and a bubble of the slab's own fill sitting in that bite, half above the edge and half below.

Two facts about that reference decided most of the implementation. **(1) Its palette is violet, and Desert Dawn has no purple.** The 2026-09-19 audit (ADR #63) had already logged M3 baseline purple leaking through an undefined `secondaryContainer` as a High finding, so the geometry was ported and the colour was re-mapped to existing tokens — no new colour was introduced for this screen. **(2) Its items are not evenly distributed across the slab: the outer items are visibly inset from the ends.** That inset is load-bearing, not decoration — the notch is centred on the active item, so an item's centre must clear the slab's corner arc by the notch's half-span plus its fillet. The reference has room to spare; this bar does not. At six items on the X6532 (720×1600 at density 320, i.e. 360dp wide) even distribution would put item 0's centre 31.6dp from the bar end while the notch needs ~41dp, which would drive the notch into the corner arc and produce a self-intersecting path (a filled artefact at the two extreme tabs).

**Decision:**

1. **Geometry, ported and then sized to the constraint.** Slab 70dp tall, 12dp side margins, 14dp corners, 8dp elevation. *(Revision 2026-09-27, ADR #92: current code reads slab 78dp, side margins 8dp — changed on later passes without a marker; #92 decision 3 records it with the fit measurement that keeps it legal.)* Bubble radius 18dp centred exactly on the bar's top edge; ring gap 2.5dp; notch fillet 4dp. One `Path` builder is shared by the shadow `Shape` (`createOutline`, so the drop shadow follows the notch) and the `drawPath` fill, and the bubble is the bar's own fill drawn on top of the notch — the gap between them is where the page shows through, which is what makes the bite read as a bite.
2. **Item centres are inset by arithmetic, never by a magic number.** The row's horizontal padding is solved so the first item's centre lands at `notchHalfSpan + corner + 2dp`: `pad = (inset·n − width/2)/(n − 1)`, evaluated from the measured width. The notch therefore cannot reach a corner at any bar width, and no device-specific constant exists to drift.
3. **Six labels are always on. This supersedes ADR #47's nav-label consequence** (its partner-pillar decisions are untouched). ADR #47 concluded that "at 360dp six labels physically cannot coexist" and set `alwaysShowLabel = false`. Measured on the device, they can coexist at 10sp with zero tracking: the widest label ("Recovery", semibold) leaves ~7dp between neighbours, and the notch's inset costs less width than the six tabs had in the first place. The user signed off on this explicitly (2026-09-22: all labels, keep six tabs). Both the size and the tracking are load-bearing — 11sp or the theme's 0.5sp tracking each push the widest label back over the line. *(Revision 2026-09-27, ADR #92: labels are 11sp in current code (user 2026-09-27: "the nav labels read too small") and still clear the 4dp guard — by 4.2dp, because the side margins narrowed from 12dp to 8dp, which buys back the extra point; measurement in #92.)*
4. **The fallback is measured, not assumed.** `rememberTextMeasurer` measures the widest label in the active (semibold) style each time tabs/typography/density change; labels are all shown only while `pitch − widest ≥ 4dp`. On a narrow phone or at a large system font scale the bar returns to selected-only on its own, which is ADR #47's behaviour rather than colliding text. That is the honest degradation, not a defect.
5. **Desert Dawn mapping, and one new CompositionLocal.** The bar is `surfaceContainerLowest` (white) in light and `surfaceContainerHigh` (elevated ink) in dark — the only surface in the app whose role differs by scheme, since no single M3 slot means "white in light, raised ink in dark". `LocalIsDarkTheme` was added to `Theme.kt` for it. Active item is `primary` + `onSurface` semibold; idle is `onSurfaceVariant`, which measures 5.07:1 on the white bar (AA). Icons follow the reference's own treatment: outlined when idle, filled when active, from the already-present `material-icons-extended` (no new dependency).
6. **Motion and semantics.** The bubble springs between item centres (`dampingRatio 0.75`, `stiffness 350`); only the icon lifts into it — item positions and the label baseline never move, which is what the reference does across its five states. No ripple; a 0.94 press scale on the icon is the feedback. Items are `Role.Tab` inside a `selectableGroup`, and a hidden label stays composed at zero alpha so it remains in the accessibility tree.

**Trade-off Accepted:** The bubble is 36dp — 0.51 of the bar's height, where the reference's is 0.73 — because a larger bubble widens the required inset and six always-on labels will not pay for both at 360dp; the reference's proportions are the casualty of the two sign-off answers. In light mode the bar is white on a near-white page, so the slab and the bubble read by shadow and by the notch curve rather than by contrast: the reference gets its separation from a saturated page, and Desert Dawn's light page has none to give. The bubble therefore carries a soft halo (black 12% fading at 1.45× radius, drawn beneath the slab) that the reference does not have — an intentional addition to keep the signature element legible on a light page. Keeping the bar white is not free styling: the audit's AA call requires it, since a warm container would drop the muted labels below 4.5:1.

**Consequences:** The bar's roundness, elevation and label weight now read as one family with the rest of Desert Dawn rather than as Material's default container, and the active destination is legible at a glance without a pill indicator. ADR #47's "six labels cannot coexist at 360dp" is retired as measured fact; a larger font scale still resolves to selected-only, so the adaptive behaviour survives as the fallback rather than as the rule. Nothing about tab identity, order, the deep-link escape hatch, or the expanded-width `NavigationRail` (which keeps full labels and now also swaps filled icons) changed — the tab list moved from `Pair<String, ImageVector>` to a `NavTab(label, icon, selectedIcon)` record purely to carry the two icon styles. The reference folder stays the source for the remaining pages of this UI pass.

---

**ADR \#75 Title: The Type Scale Steps by Window Class, Not by Screen — One Theme-Level Decision Plus a Source Guard for Screens Not Yet Written (User standing rule, 2026-09-22)**

**Status:** Accepted; user-mandated 2026-09-22 ("i also hope that all features pages etc etc text even can adjust size based on screen size" · "the scaking is for all pages even future ones"), built, JVM-tested (`ScaledTypographyTest`, `NoLiteralTypeSizeTest`), device-verified at COMPACT on the X6532.

**Context:** The owner's rule is that text adapts to the screen on **every** page, including pages that do not exist yet. The codebase already had the seam: `rememberWindowWidth()` (`ui/Adaptive.kt`) classifies the window from `LocalWindowInfo.current.containerSize` into COMPACT (<600dp) / MEDIUM (<840dp) / EXPANDED, and `AppShell` already caps content at `ContentMaxWidth = 840.dp`. Nothing used it for type.

The trap that decided the shape of this ADR: a step applied in `Theme.kt` reaches only text that reads a typography role. Any surface that declares a literal `fontSize = 13.sp` opts itself out — silently, including screens written after this ADR. "All pages, even future ones" therefore cannot be delivered by the theme change alone.

**Decision:**

1. **One step at the theme.** `MyExodusTheme` derives its typography from the window class: COMPACT = base verbatim; MEDIUM = ×1.125; EXPANDED = ×1.25. `scaledTypography` walks all fifteen roles, so dialogs, sheets and not-yet-written screens inherit the step with no per-screen code.
2. **A step, not a proportion.** The factor depends on the class, not on the measured width: a phone that grows 4dp does not reflow its text, and the sizes stay round numbers a designer can name.
3. **Lengths stay `sp`.** `fontSize`, `lineHeight` and `letterSpacing` all scale and are re-labelled `sp`, so the user's accessibility font scale still multiplies on top. Converting to dp would freeze text at the current density and silently disable that setting. Non-sp units pass through untouched — an em-based style is never re-labelled sp (pinned by test).
4. **The opt-out is mechanically forbidden.** `NoLiteralTypeSizeTest` scans `src/main/java/com/example/myexodus/ui` (excluding `ui/theme`) for `fontSize`/`lineHeight` literals in `sp`/`em` and fails listing the offenders. This is what extends the rule to pages not yet written: a future screen that hardcodes a size fails the suite rather than shipping.
5. **`ScaledTypographyTest` pins the arithmetic** — every role scales exactly, factor 1.0 is the identity, em lengths are never re-labelled.

**Trade-off Accepted:** The guard is a source-text scan, not a compiler check: it catches literal sizes but not a size smuggled through a variable (`fontSize = x.sp`). Accepted with the ceiling named — the upgrade path is a lint rule if that ever bites. Second, a large system font scale on a small screen now wraps more text than before on MEDIUM windows; accepted, since the alternative is ignoring the window class entirely. Third, 1.125/1.25 are chosen constants; a proportional scale would be smoother but would double-count the accessibility font scale and produce sizes nobody designed.

**Consequences:** `ui/theme/Type.kt` is now the only place in the app where a text size exists, and every screen — present and future — takes the window step for free. The standing rule is enforced by the suite rather than by memory, which is what the user asked for ("even future ones"). MEDIUM and EXPANDED are covered by the unit-test arithmetic; the on-device `wm size`/`wm density` screenshot check of those two classes was **pending at the time of writing** (device busy) rather than performed, and is tracked on the session task list.

---

**ADR \#76 Title: The Home Dashboard Takes the Reference's Structure — Date/Greeting Header, Three-Tile Stat Row, 90-Day Milestone Grid — and Refuses Its Deltas, Charts and Always-On CTA for Lack of Data (2026-09-22)**

**Status:** Accepted; user-approved 2026-09-22 in two rounds (structure, then the header anchor), built, JVM-tested, device-verified on the X6532 at 360dp in dark (loaded and pre-first-emission loading states). The light-theme re-capture and the MEDIUM/EXPANDED passes were interrupted by an incoming call on the test device and are **pending**, not done.

**Context:** Same process, skill and reference folder as ADR #74 (`/Users/musila/Documents/ReferenceInspirationUI`; the online-research step was waived for this UI pass only). The leading study is a light workout dashboard: muted eyebrow + big title, a chips row, a full-width CTA, a 3-up stat trio carrying deltas, a 30-day dot grid, two chart cards and detail rows.

The binding constraint turned out to be the data contract, not the design. `HabitState` carries `currentStreakElapsedMs`, `allTimeMaxElapsedMs`, `currentDay`, `milestones`, severity and withdrawal weeks — **no per-day history**. The reference's stat deltas (+8 % / +3 % / +41 %), its 30-day grid and both chart cards all require history that does not exist, so they were refused *before* sign-off rather than approximated. What is honest at 90 days is the ladder itself: `MilestoneEvent.MILESTONE_DAYS = listOf(7, 30, 90)`, the single source of truth for milestones.

**Decision:**

1. **Header = date eyebrow + state pill on one row, then a greeting as the page's anchor** (second-round sign-off; the first-round "Today" eyebrow read as bare). The eyebrow is the real date — `formatHeaderDate`, pattern `"EEEE, d MMMM"`, no year, because the year is dead weight on a screen about the current streak and no localized `FormatStyle` drops it. The greeting is time-of-day only (morning 5–11, afternoon 12–17, else evening); late hours read "Good evening" rather than "Good night", which is a farewell in English, not a greeting. Neither element can contradict the protection state — only the pill claims anything.
2. **The reference's subtitle, chips row and always-on CTA are dropped.** A subtitle is filler; a chips row would restate the stat trio 16dp below it; an always-on action has no honest MyExodus equivalent on this screen.
3. **Stat trio: best streak · blocks · blocked.** Best streak is `habitDays(allTimeMaxElapsedMs)` — the habit-level record across all streaks, so it survives both a habit switch and a relapse. Every tile renders a skeleton while its value is `null` and never flashes a zero a moment before the real count arrives; the best-streak tile is the one that can render while the streak itself is still unresolved.
4. **The 90-day grid (30 columns × 3 rows) is the only honest long-range visual.** Fill is `habitDays(currentStreakElapsedMs)` — the same value the hero renders, so the two can never disagree (the plan's state-coherence rule, line 39). Rings mark 7/30/90 from `MILESTONE_DAYS`; a filled cell is gold (`tertiary`) only when that day is in the active streak's achieved milestones, and primary teal otherwise. With no habit the card is absent rather than empty, since the hero above already carries the fix.
5. **Scope honesty inside the trio:** best streak is habit-scoped and follows a habit switch; "Blocks" and "Blocked" are protection-scoped and deliberately do not — rules belong to the profile, not to a streak. "Blocked" is all-time (`GROUP BY blocked_target` over the whole `attempt_log`) and `attempt_log` carries no profile column, so it cannot be profile-scoped at all: correct today with exactly one local profile, latent the day a second ships.
6. **Chip strings moved to resources — for real.** Finding #13's Fix column in `ui-ux-improvement-session.md` claims "chip strings moved to resources", but `HomeScreen.kt` still held Kotlin literals; the claim was true of the plan, not of the code. Now true (`protection_state_*`). Same class of divergence the plan-fidelity protocol §5 exists to catch: the plan is verified against the code, not against other docs.

**Trade-off Accepted:** Card-on-page contrast measures ≈1.05 in our palette (card `surfaceContainerLow` #F6F2EC on page #FAF8F5) against the reference's ≈1.09 — measured by pixel-sampling both images, not eyeballed. Left as-is: the card tone is used in ~44 places app-wide, and this page's separation comes from the teal hero, the trio and the grid rather than from card edges. Recorded as an accepted deviation instead of a silent one.

**Consequences:** Home now has a real reading order — date, protection verdict, greeting, record, ladder, rules — with the streak numeral as the page's single loudest element and no invented number anywhere on it. Two honest absences are load-bearing rather than gaps: no milestone grid without a habit, and no stat trio values before the first emission. What is **not** covered: any per-day or per-week trend (impossible without history), and the two block-scoped tiles remain profile-wide by construction, which the trio's labels do not currently disclose individually.

---

**ADR \#77 Title: The Recovery Page Splits into a Hub Plus Icon+Label Subpages — One Suggested Technique Leads, Reference Material Moves Behind Tiles, the Crisis Path Never Does (2026-09-22)**

**Status:** Accepted; user-approved 2026-09-22 (scope "Recovery now, Partner next"; hub keeps one recommended technique), built, JVM suite green (**1040 / 0 / 0 / 0**). **Device-verified 2026-09-22.** The gate this line carried — "an `install -r` would invalidate the battery day-log" — was false and is retired rather than restated: the sampler is a detached device-side shell loop at `/data/local/tmp/battery-day-log.sh`, so package replacement never touched it, and the thing it measures was already down (every log row `vpn=STOPPED`, FGS `isForeground=false`). Recovery hub confirmed on the X6532 at 360dp COMPACT dark: 5 in-page targets ("Paced breathing", "Log an urge", the Techniques/My plan/History 3-up sharing one y, "Need urgent help?") — the hub keeps the crisis path and the one live action, exactly as designed.

**Context:** Owner ruling, verbatim: *"if u see a page with soo much we can split them into subpages with their own icons … but i dont want to see an entire page dedicated to one thing like a one toggle … this is just an example dont limit yourself to this think critically"*. Recovery was named as the example, not the scope. Measured against the rule, Recovery's idle state rendered **8 blocks** (title/subtitle, state card, four stacked technique cards, coaching plan, action row, crisis card) — the four stacked cards and the plan section carrying nearly all of the visual weight. The plan's own research digest supplies the constraint that keeps such a split honest: primary action ≤2 taps, and emergency/escape access is never gated.

**Decision:**

1. **Only reference material moves.** The hub keeps the live state (timer or idle card), the log-urge action as a full-width button, and the crisis card. The full catalog, the coaching plan and history move behind three icon+label tiles (Techniques · My plan · History). The mid-urge path is unchanged: the suggested technique is still one tap, logging is still one tap, and crisis help is still on the screen the user is already looking at. Opening a *non*-suggested technique costs one extra tap (tile, then card) — stated here rather than glossed.
2. **One suggestion leads, and it is the shipped rule, not a new one.** `RecoveryResourceCatalog.recommended(rating = null, inRiskWindow)` finally has its first UI caller; until now the function shipped with v2 M1.3 and was invoked only by its own unit tests. Inside a risk window it leads with the low-effort grounding technique, otherwise the guided walkthrough. `rating` is null because nothing on this surface rates an urge before the log sheet exists. The Techniques tile *is* the "choose another" path that function's own contract promises.
3. **Subpages are reached by icon + label, and the label is the destination's own string.** Tiles render `recovery_tile_techniques` (new), `recovery_plan_title` ("My plan") and `recovery_history_title` ("History") — the same keys the destinations already used, so a rename cannot desynchronise a tile from the page it opens.
4. **Tiles use Material's clickable `Surface`, not a bare `clickable`.** Each tile is icon + label rather than one datum, so the clickable overload (merged semantics for TalkBack, 48dp interactive minimum) is the platform's own answer; the icon carries `contentDescription = null` because the label names the control.
5. **Routing reuses the screen's existing view state machine** (`rememberSaveable` string view + `BackRow`), so the split adds two constants and two branches, not a second navigation system. Both new subpages are scrollable and keep the screen's window-width side padding (ADR #75's COMPACT/MEDIUM step).
6. **A subpage that carries one control is never created.** The rule's second edge is binding on the next candidates too: Partner's paired state (up to 7 cards) is the next split by the owner's own ordering, and the audit lives in `ui-ux-improvement-session.md` rather than in this entry.

**Trade-off Accepted:** The idle hub now renders 6 blocks against the previous 8 — a real reduction in weight (four stacked cards plus the plan section collapse to one card plus one tile row), but not a dramatic drop in block *count*. The remainder is deliberate: every block left on the hub is either the live state, the live action, or the crisis path, each of which the digest's rules forbid moving behind a tap. If the count still reads as heavy on device, the split should deepen (a category grouping inside Techniques) rather than push live state behind a tile.

**Consequences:** Recovery reads as one decision (what to do now) with its reference material one tap away, and the "one recommended technique" behaviour is now a shipped UI rule backed by an already-tested catalog function instead of a test-only primitive. The dangerous regression this split could have introduced — an extra tap in front of help — is structurally prevented, not merely avoided. What is **not** covered: no instrumentation test for the tile routing (this screen has no Compose UI-test harness, and the routing is string-keyed), so the verification for it is the device pass; and the partner split is recorded, not built.

---

**ADR \#78 Title: The Partner Tab Splits into a Lean Hub Plus Requests · Activity · Pairing — the Hub Answers Only "Does Anything Need Me?", and a Degraded Sync Still Comes Back to It (2026-09-22)**

**Status:** Accepted; user-approved 2026-09-22 (option "Lean hub"; the notifications banner stays on the hub in every state), built, JVM suite green (**1040 / 0 / 0 / 0**). **Device-verified 2026-09-22** — the "pending" gate this line used to carry was false: the battery sampler is a detached device-side shell loop, so an `install -r` never touched it, and the services it was said to protect were already down (`vpn=STOPPED`, FGS `isForeground=false`). Installed over wireless adb in 9.4s with grants surviving. Partner hub confirmed live on the X6532: 4 in-page targets — "Check now" plus the Activity/Pairing/Requests tiles — in the degraded branch that keeps `SyncCard`. See the plan's §Device pass.

**Context:** Second application of the owner's dense-page ruling (ADR #77), against the audit line already recorded: the paired state renders **up to 7 cards** (chain status, approvals, identity, sync, activity, my requests, unpair). Three facts from reading the screen decided the design rather than the card count alone:

1. `ChainStatusCard` renders **nothing when the chain is healthy** — it is a needs-attention-only card (`HEALTHY -> return`). So the common paired hub carried no verdict at all; the weight was entirely reference material.
2. The other pair states — **unpaired, wipe-detected, ended** — already render a single card each. The rule's second edge ("never a page for one control") forbids splitting those, so the split is scoped to the paired state and nothing else.
3. `SyncCard` is the AD-05 staleness surface, and ADR-pinned honesty says a visual pass must never soften or hide it. Moving it behind a tile was therefore only permissible with a mechanism that brings it back.

**Decision:**

1. **The hub answers one question — "does anything need me?"** It keeps the chain verdict (needs-attention only, per fact 1), the pending-approval card while one is time-boxed, and the tile row. Identity, the sync detail, the activity list and unpair are reference material and moved behind **Requests · Activity · Pairing**.
2. **A degraded sync returns to the hub** (`checkFailed || syncNeedsAttention(state)`, where the predicate is never-synced **or** `STALE` **or** rate-limited). The full card — including the manual **Check now** — always lives on the Pairing page; the hub shows it only when it has something the user may have to act on, and a healthy sync stays quiet there. This is what makes decision 1 honest rather than a silent hide.
3. **Requests is the whole ask record:** the card awaiting your decision (the same component the hub renders while it is time-boxed, from the same `approvals` list, so the two surfaces cannot disagree), then the status of the asks you sent, then an empty state. **Activity** is the existing activity card verbatim. **Pairing** is identity, sync detail, and unpair — destructive last and low-emphasis, which is also the reason it is not on the hub.
4. **A subpage owns the screen.** The page header and the notifications banner yield to the subpage's `BackRow`; the banner is a degradation banner and stays on the hub in every state as the user specified, it is not duplicated into subpages.
5. **The unpair dialog was hoisted out of the paired branch.** It has to survive the state change confirming it causes — inside the old branch, the dialog's own success would have removed it mid-flight and taken the failure message with it (the failure path renders in the page, which is also where the button now is).
6. **Shared chrome extracted to `ui/`** (`BackRow.kt`, `NavTile.kt`) — the split gave both a second consumer, and `BackRow` already existed in three copies (one private, two inline). Recovery was switched to the shared pair; `CoachingPlanScreen.kt` and `RiskWindowsScreen.kt` still carry their inline copies and were deliberately **not** touched (unrelated to this split, and unverifiable on device right now). Converting them is a mechanical follow-up.

**Trade-off Accepted:** **Check now** is one tap deeper in the common case — the button sits on the hub only while the sync is degraded, and otherwise lives on the Pairing page. Accepted because a manual refresh is not time-critical, unlike the approval decision that stayed on the hub. The pending-approval card also now appears on two surfaces (hub while time-boxed, Requests page always); accepted deliberately — both read the same list, so a contradiction is structurally impossible, and the page is a superset rather than a second source.

**Consequences:** The paired hub drops from up to 7 cards to **2 cards plus the tile row** in the healthy case, and the screen's own documented hierarchy (needs-attention first) is now the layout instead of a comment — everything that could silently mislead is on the hub, everything that merely informs is one tap away. The one honesty rule the split could have broken (AD-05 staleness) is enforced by the `syncNeedsAttention` predicate rather than by remembering to check. What is **not** covered: no instrumentation test for the tile routing (same ceiling as ADR #77 — string-keyed routing, no Compose UI-test harness on this screen), so the device pass is its only verification; and the two inline `BackRow` copies noted above.

---

**ADR \#79 Title: Streaks Reaches the Four-Week Timeline Through a Row, Not Inline — and the Same Row Retires SettingsScreen's Private Duplicate (2026-09-22)**

**Status:** Accepted; user-approved 2026-09-22 (page pick "Streaks"; the card's fate: "Move behind a spoke"), built, JVM suite green (**1040 / 0 / 0 / 0**). **Device-verified 2026-09-22** on the X6532 at 360dp COMPACT in dark: the hub, the `VIEW_TIMELINE` spoke (full timeline card, "You are here" on Week 1, four bars on **taupe** tracks — finding #9's fix holds, no purple, Decision 12's disclaimer present) and the back route. The battery-day-log gate this line used to carry was false; see ADR #78's status for why, and the plan's §Device pass.

**Context:** Third application of the owner's dense-page ruling (ADR #77), and the first where the reference folder itself did **not** survive contact with the data model. The supplied references for this screen (`13.05.58` — week day-dots, celebration header, Claim CTA; `a6d7fc70` — eyebrow, big number, period tabs, labelled bar chart, 2×2 %-grid) were extracted before anything was offered, and most of their structure had to be refused on code facts rather than taste:

1. **The Mon–Sun week row is calendar math, which the streak engine forbids.** `HabitState.recoveryStartAt` carries the comment *"Day counts derive from `currentStreakElapsedMs`, not from this (Decision 17: no calendar math)"*. The streak is a 24-hour accumulator; there is also no per-day held record, so a week row could only restate the hero's day count across seven cells.
2. **The labelled bar chart would dress a static estimate as measurement.** `withdrawalWeeks` is `WithdrawalCurve` — the same shape for every user, and the card's own detail line already says so (`TIMELINE_DISCLAIMER`, Decision 12: *"a general estimate to help set expectations — not a clinical measurement"*). Per-bar values are exactly the claim that disclaimer exists to refuse.
3. **The 2×2 %-grid and the 7-day/Month/Year tabs need history that does not exist.** No denominators, no per-day rows. Each would have been an invented number — the same class ADR #76 refused for Home.
4. **The Claim CTA already ships** as `MilestoneSplashCard` ("Nice — keep going"), the REQ-12 guaranteed-delivery path.

So the screen's real problem was not its structure but its **weight**: the four-week card rendered ~13 content lines (four bars, five paragraphs, the disclaimer) and, by its own code comment, the bars only order the weeks. It was the heaviest block on the page carrying the least live state.

**Decision:**

1. **The four-week card moves behind a row.** The hub keeps everything that changes while the user watches — the hero (`StreakSummaryCard`), the milestone ladder with its live countdowns (`MilestoneLadderCard`), and the relapse action (`RelapseCard`). "What to expect" becomes a `LinkRow` opening a `VIEW_TIMELINE` spoke that renders `WithdrawalTimelineCard` unchanged, disclaimer included.
2. **The row says what the material is, before the tap.** Its body is *"A general estimate of the first four weeks — not a clinical measurement."* — Decision 12's disclaimer reaches the hub instead of being discovered only on arrival. Moving the card does **not** weaken that rule: the card still carries `TIMELINE_DISCLAIMER` everywhere it renders, which is now exactly one place.
3. **The spoke needs a habit, so it falls back rather than strands.** `state?.takeIf { view == VIEW_TIMELINE }` — a habit that disappears while the spoke is open returns the user to the hub instead of leaving a `BackRow` over an empty page. (Same class of bug ADR #78 had to solve for the unpair dialog: state that unmounts the surface that mutated it.) The predicate is deliberately **only** that precondition: an earlier draft also gated on `!addingHabit` for an "add habit started underneath the spoke" path that cannot be reached (the sheet opens from the hub), and that clause was the *worse* of the two — a `view` left at `timeline` across a setup would have re-entered the spoke the moment a replacement habit landed.
4. **A single destination gets a row, not a tile.** `NavTile` is a unit of a *set* — a lone square tile in a one-item row reads as a broken grid. `LinkRow` (title + body, full-width, Material clickable `Surface` for merged semantics and the 48dp minimum) is the single-destination counterpart.
5. **`LinkRow` is the promoted `SettingsScreen.SettingsLinkRow`, not a copy.** The private composable already had that exact shape and two consumers; Streaks made it three and a shared home. `ui/LinkRow.kt` now carries it, Settings' two recovery rows call the shared version, and the private duplicate is deleted. Its one deliberate delta: `Surface(onClick = …)` replaces `Modifier.clickable(…)`, so those two Settings rows gain the merged TalkBack button and the 48dp minimum, and their ripple clips to the card's rounded shape.

**The 13.05.58 structures that were refused, and why each refusal is recorded rather than deferred:** the week row (Decision 17), the labelled bars (Decision 12), the %-grid and period tabs (no data). These are not "future polish" — the first would require amending Decision 17, and the second would require `WithdrawalCurve` to become per-user measured data. Either is a product change, not a visual one.

**Trade-off Accepted:** The page's **block count does not drop** — five before, five after (title, hero, ladder, row, relapse) — because the ladder stays, having live countdowns. The win is weight and reading order, not count: a ~13-line card becomes a 2-line row. Stated plainly because "the hub got shorter" would be false here in a way it was not for Recovery (8→6) or Partner (7→2).

**Consequences:** Streaks now reads as a live screen with one piece of reading material attached, and the four-week card is reached deliberately by a user who wants to read it. What is **not** covered: no instrumentation test for the spoke routing (same ceiling as ADR #77/#78 — string-keyed `rememberSaveable` routing, no Compose UI-test harness on this screen), so the device pass is its only verification; the timeline card's bars keep their existing behaviour and documentation ("bar length orders the weeks; it is not a score") rather than being redesigned, since only the card's *position* was in scope. A separate pre-existing finding stands untouched: this screen's copy is still hardcoded Kotlin literals throughout (`StreaksScreen`, `StreakCards`, `WithdrawalTimelineCard`), the same defect class finding #13 corrected for Home's status chip; `LinkRow`'s copy followed the screen's local convention rather than half-migrating the file, and converting all of it is a follow-up.

---

**ADR \#80 Title: INV-11 Closes Where a Comment Said It Already Had — the Coaching Editor, the Risk Windows and the History Each Own Their `SecureSurface` (2026-09-22)**

**Status:** Accepted; built and device-verified 2026-09-22 on the X6532. JVM suite green (**1040 / 0 / 0 / 0** at the time of the build; **1051 / 0 / 0 / 0** across 135 classes with this session's other work).

**Context:** Finding #29, from the cognitive-load pass's device probe. `SecureSurface()` had exactly **one** call site — `RecoveryScreen.kt:197` — while the KDoc on `SecureSurfaces` asserted three ("the recovery tab, the session history, and the coaching editor each [SecureSurface] themselves"). The claim was a comment; nothing enforced it. Measured on the X6532 rather than reasoned about:

1. **Coaching plan** (Settings → Coaching plan) — no SECURE window; `screencap` returned **91,265 bytes** of live editor content.
2. **Risk windows** (Settings → Risk windows) — no SECURE window; `screencap` returned **70,358 bytes**.
3. **Session history** — protected only *incidentally*: it renders inside the Recovery tab's composition, so `RecoveryScreen`'s count stayed above zero (capture 0 bytes, biometric gate fired). Protection that depends on the route you took to reach a screen is not protection.

Both exposed surfaces are **mental-health content reachable from the Settings tab**, where no recovery surface is mounted above them. The comment is why this survived review: a reader sees coverage asserted and stops looking.

**Decision:**

1. **Each of the three surfaces declares its own `SecureSurface()`** — `CoachingPlanScreen.kt:68`, `RiskWindowsScreen.kt:70`, `SessionHistoryScreen.kt:65` — placed at the top of each composable, alongside the pre-existing call in `RecoveryScreen`. The invariant M2.8 already stated ("every recovery activity/composable host sets `FLAG_SECURE`") is now what the code does, not what a comment hoped.
2. **The refcount mechanism is untouched, and nesting stays fine.** `SecureSurfaces` counts composed secure surfaces and `MainActivity` renders `>0` as `FLAG_SECURE`; the History now contributes its own count on top of the hub's, which is harmless because the value is a count, not a flag. No per-screen flag, no priority, no owner — the design that made the nesting safe is the one that made the fix a one-liner per surface.
3. **The KDoc is corrected to describe the call sites as they are**, and to name why the defect existed at all: *"A stale comment claiming all three already declared it is why that survived review."* A corrected comment is not a fix, but a comment that repeats the false claim is how the next reader gets misled.
4. **The duplicate-per-surface rule is deliberate, not defensive.** An alternative — declaring `SecureSurface()` once at the tab host — was rejected on the terms the fix itself establishes: the Settings tab hosts two recovery panes with no recovery surface above them, so host-level declaration would have reproduced exactly the route-dependent protection this ADR removes.

**Trade-off Accepted:** Four declarations where one *looked* like enough. The cost is that a new recovery surface can forget its own declaration, and nothing today fails when it does — which is the honest limit of this fix, recorded below rather than papered over.

**Consequences:** The two Settings panes are no longer screenshot- or screen-recording-exposed; `screencap` on both returns **0 bytes** on device (the same signature Recovery has always had), and the History's protection no longer depends on which route reached it. What is **not** covered: there is no test that fails when a future recovery surface omits `SecureSurface()` — the invariant is enforced by reviewers reading four call sites, which is exactly the defence that failed here. The durable answer is a source-scanning guard test, the same class as `NoLiteralTypeSizeTest` and `SecureSurfacesTest`, and it is tracked as its own task (the cross-cutting guard-test pass, task #29) rather than claimed as done. `SecureSurfacesTest` covers the counter's arithmetic and the window-flag mapping; it cannot see a missing declaration.

---

**ADR \#81 Title: History Says What It Counts — the "Completed / Ended Early" Switch Becomes Three Buckets and an Honest Range, Because Neither Word Was One a User Would Ask With (2026-09-22)**

**Status:** Accepted; user-approved 2026-09-22 (option "Three-way, no switch"), built, JVM-tested (`HistorySummaryTest`), device-verified on the X6532 at 360dp COMPACT.

**Context:** The owner's own reading of the screen, verbatim: *"what the hell does w m completed ended early all reference or mean heck what does the history page show? what its purpose as a user i dont know what it does"*. Three separate defects in one card:

1. **`W` / `M` were initials with no legend** — abbreviations invented by the reference's `13.10.55` layout (a D/W/M/Y income/spending switcher) and carried across without the legend that made them readable there.
2. **"Ended early" existed only because the schema needed a folder.** It merged `STATE_ABANDONED` ("Stopped") with `STATE_INTERRUPTED` ("Interrupted") — a word chosen for the data model's convenience, describing which row changed rather than what changed for the user.
3. **The worst one: a bare log counted as a completed session.** `RecoverySessionRepository.logOnly` writes `STATE_COMPLETED` with technique `none`, so a summary keyed on state alone told the user "3 completed" while the list one row below labelled the same row *"Logged, no technique"*. Two surfaces, same moment, contradicting — the plan's state-coherence rule (line 39).

**Decision:**

1. **The switch is gone; three buckets replace it.** `completed` (a technique was practised, to the end) · `notFinished` (Stopped *or* Interrupted, merged deliberately — the row still says which, and at summary level the only question is whether it ran to the end) · `loggedOnly` (a bare log: no technique attempted). The buckets distinguish **what changed for the user**, not what changed in the row.
2. **The range control stays, spelled out.** `W/M/All` becomes `Week / Month / All time`. The reference's D/W/M/Y was refused on data: a day of personal urge logs is typically 0–3 rows, so a daily bucket is noise, and a year bucket is empty for nearly every V1 install. Both ends of the reference's range were removed for the same reason — there is no honest data under them.
3. **`STARTED` rows are excluded from all three.** A session still running has no outcome yet; counting it either way would be a claim about the future.
4. **The summary scopes only itself.** The reference's Income/Spending switch filters its whole view, but those are disjoint categories of money — these are readings of *one* log, and hiding a relapse from the record is the wrong affordance in a recovery app. The log below stays the full chronological record. List filtering is a small add if it is ever wanted; it is deliberately not built.
5. **The reference's chart below the chips is refused** for the same reason ADR #76 and ADR #79 refused theirs: there is no per-day history to plot. The three buckets are the honest substitute, and they reuse Home's stat-trio shape so the app has one way of showing a set of counts.
6. **Zeros still render.** Every bucket is drawn on every render including zeros, because a label that appears only once it has a count cannot be compared across periods. On an empty window the figure block is absent but the range chips are not — "no urges in this period" is itself a claim about the period, and it needs the control that says which period.

**Trade-off Accepted:** `loggedOnly` is a third bucket rather than a footnote, which makes the card three items wide on a 360dp screen. The alternative — folding bare logs into "completed", which is what the code previously did — was rejected outright: it is the contradiction this ADR exists to remove.

**Consequences:** The page now answers "what does this show" without the user having to infer it from two unexplained words, and the summary can no longer disagree with the list below it. `summarize()` and `windowStartMs()` are pure and unit-tested without a clock; `HistorySummaryTest` covers the logOnly/state split and the window boundary. What is **not** covered: the range chips re-query on each tap but the JVM test asserts the bucketing and the window arithmetic, not the composition — the routing and rendering rest on the device pass. Device evidence (X6532, 360dp COMPACT): eyebrow *"Urges logged"* at y=240–273, figure **5** at y=297–385, all three chips on one shared baseline at y=425–458, and the bucket row at y=498–582 reading **Completed 2 · Not finished 3 · Logged only 0** — the zero rendered, as designed. This surface is behind the privacy gate and declares `SecureSurface` (ADR #80), so `screencap` returns 0 bytes by design and the a11y tree is the verification channel.

---

**ADR \#82 Title: Settings Becomes an Index — One Row Per Thing, the Six Permission Rows Move Behind "Protection", and About Finally Exists to Carry the Disclaimer Three Documents Already Claimed Had Shipped (2026-09-23)**

**Status:** Accepted; user-approved 2026-09-23 (option "Index + Protection subpage"), built, JVM suite green (**1051 / 0 / 0 / 0** across 135 classes), device-verified on the X6532 at 360dp COMPACT in both themes.

**Context:** Two forces met on this screen.

**The owner's dense-page ruling** (ADR #77/#78/#79) reached Settings last, and Settings is the case where the rule's *second* edge — never a page for one control — does real work: of the six sections the screen carried, two (Discreet widget, Crash reports) were a single toggle each, and the Enhanced Mode section was exactly two. Splitting those into subpages would have produced three pages holding one control apiece. The other force was the row count: Settings was a tab root whose own content was **six stacked sections plus four subpage links**, and the cognitive-load probe measured it at 6 in-page choices / 2.58 bits / **28 text chunks** — the second-highest chunk count in the app after Home, on a screen whose job is to be scanned.

The reference is `13.12.54` (settings list: icon + label + chevron rows, grouped, sub-values inline). It maps 1:1, with one structural refusal: the reference is a bare list of rows directly on the page background, and MyExodus already has an app-wide card convention (`surfaceContainerLow` + `shapes.medium`) that Settings had been using for sections. A single card of rows was chosen over the reference's bare list to keep that convention (see deviations, below).

**Decision:**

1. **One row per thing the user can check or change, and every destination is one tap.** The index renders one card of **eight rows**: Protection · Enhanced Mode · Strict Mode · Discreet widget · Coaching plan · Risk windows · Crash reports · About. Switches toggle in place; destinations open a pane. The one invariant: a row either *is* the control or *opens* the control.
2. **The six permission/enforcement rows move behind the Protection row** — the only rows carrying a live status word and an action, which is precisely what earns a subpage. The index row carries the verdict instead: *"Everything we can check is on"* or the pluralised *"N settings need attention"*, with a status dot whose tone mirrors the subpage.
3. **`attentionCount` is the index row's claim, so it is a tested function, not an inline expression.** It counts the five mechanisms that can be wrong — service disabled, usage access, battery exemption, overlay, and integrity **only when it has actually failed** (unverified and unavailable are informational; the OEM row never reports). `ProtectionSummaryTest` guards the arithmetic in both directions: too high is a false alarm, too low hides a broken mechanism behind a reassuring number. **Null until the checks land**: the row shows no status rather than claiming one it hasn't read.
4. **The About surface is built, because three separate documents already claimed it shipped.** Recovery plan v2 M1.5 and v3 both require a *"self-help tool, not medical treatment"* footer, `MyExodus V1.md:881` states the footer *"stands"* on Settings → About, and no About screen and no such string existed anywhere in the codebase. It now renders BrandMark, the app name, `Version %1$s`, and the disclaimer — closing a documented-but-unbuilt requirement rather than a UI preference.
5. **The unsupported-configuration case stops hiding the feature.** Under Advanced Protection the old code `return`ed a whole section, so the user could not see that Enhanced Mode exists but is unavailable. Both switches now render **disabled with the reason as the row's subtitle** — the user is told why, and told the feature is real.
6. **The restricted-settings hint moves to the Protection subpage**, where the accessibility row it explains actually lives, instead of sitting on an index that no longer shows that row.
7. **Two more unnamed switches were found and fixed on the way past.** `Discreet widget` and `Crash reports` had the same defect class as finding #26 (a `Switch` whose accessible name carries no subject); both now announce their own row title. This is the same class ADR #79's `LinkRow` promotion addressed, and the durable guard is the source-scanning test pass (task #29) rather than a helper invented for two call sites.
