**Verified.** JVM, new `MailboxOutboxDrainerCrashWindowTest` (9 tests, green) against a stateful fake of the real semantics (doc id = message id; a second write to an id throws `ALREADY_EXISTS`, exactly like the create-only rules gate): kill-after-write-before-mark full replay delivers each id exactly once and marks every row (latency sampled per real write only); mid-chain partial replay preserves order; crafted crash-state resume mixes replays and one fresh row in one batch; **the fix's pins** — a `markSent` throw after a fresh write and on the ALREADY_EXISTS path both leave rows PENDING while `drain()` still returns `Sent(n)`, and the next pass heals with zero duplicate writes; attempt-log throws under UNAVAILABLE / PERMISSION_DENIED / generic failures never change the decided result; transient mid-batch failure stops the pass and the replay delivers exactly once in order; denied mid-batch leaves k..n pending until a recovery pass; 250 rows drain in 100/100/50 chunks. Existing `MailboxOutboxDrainerTest` (8) unchanged, green. Full JVM suite green after every campaign main-source change: 185 classes / 1,618 tests, 0 failures (2026-10-14).

**Honest limits.** (a) Process death is simulated: the fake models Firestore's create-only + doc-id identity (the exact model the rules tests pin), but no on-device kill-mid-drain capture was taken tonight — the window is a 15-minute worker cadence, and landing a kill inside a 100-row batch on the X6532 was not attempted. (b) `bookkeeping` logs only — no telemetry counter; the still-PENDING row next pass is the observable. (c) A persistently failing mark delays that pair's pull by one pass per attempt; while the disk is full the pull would likely be failing anyway.

## #148 — Seams for the Mailbox Receiver: the Watermark Machinery Gets Its First Tests (2026-10-14)

**Context.** The stress campaign's A8 slice (mailbox watermark / foreign-document adversarials) found **zero product bugs** — its value is coverage — but there was no receiver-level coverage to build on. `FirestoreMailboxReceiver` held a `FirebaseFirestore` directly and reached for collections inline (the mirrored wake fetch, the watermark-ordered query with `startAfter` + limit 500, the server-timestamp conversion), so every watermark behavior was only exercisable against real Firestore. Separately, the ADR #46 harness seam (open `agreementPrivateKey*` so the virtual partner can substitute its attested test key) was structurally unusable in JVM tests: the class's field initializer `KeyStore.getInstance("AndroidKeyStore").apply { load(null) }` runs at every subclass construction, and the JVM test environment has no AndroidKeyStore provider (Robolectric 4.16 registers none — jar-inspected after the first test run failed on exactly this).

**Decision.**
1. `FirestoreMailboxReceiver` takes two Firestore-access seams in the house pattern (`MailboxOutboxDrainer`'s rationale, verbatim): `queryMailbox(myUid, afterEpochMillis, limit)` and `fetchMessage(myUid, messageId)`. The Firestore-shaped code moves to `FirestoreSchema` — `mailboxDocsAfter` (the watermark query + snapshot decode), `mailboxDoc` (the wake-id fetch), and a shared `mailboxDocumentOf`; `timestampOf` moves with its microsecond-truncation comment. Receiver behavior is byte-identical; the DI provider (`AccountabilityModule`) and the virtual-partner harness (`PartnerTestIdentity`, debug source set) bind the schema functions — the only two construction sites.
2. `AccountabilityKeyStore`'s backing `KeyStore` becomes `by lazy` — no behavior change on device (the provider exists; first touch is a key operation either way), but harness subclasses now construct off-device, completing the ADR #46 seam instead of leaving it device-only.

**Alternatives considered.** (a) *Register a stub AndroidKeyStore provider in the test* — a ~40-line SPI shim to spare a one-word production change; the lazy initializer makes the seam honest everywhere rather than in the one rig that assembled a shim. (b) *A Robolectric keystore shadow* — none ships in 4.16, and adding a dependency for one test rig fails the ladder. (c) *Reflection to neutralize the field* — pokes private state, breaks on rename, and teaches the next reader nothing. (d) *Keep session-level coverage only (status quo)* — that is the gap being closed.

**Verified.** JVM, new `MailboxWatermarkStressTest` (13 tests, green) over the real Room `AccountabilityDatabase`/`ChainStateStore`, real `CekDerivation` ECDH, real `SessionKeyManager` policy, and the real `MailboxMessageHandler` with fake seams; the fake mailbox models Firestore's actual semantics (orderBy excludes null-`timestamp` docs, `startAfter` is strictly greater, limit applies after ordering) so the tests pin receiver behavior, not fake conveniences. Pins: ascending bulk handling with one max-watermark persist (and the epoch-0 envelope passing the real receive policy on a headless chain); a late sub-watermark arrival invisible to bulk and recovered only by its wake id, without regressing the watermark; foreign (other-pair and null-pair) docs advance the watermark without delivering and are never re-read — and a wake for one still runs the bulk; an unsorted page never regresses the watermark; an unchanged watermark is not re-persisted; a null-timestamp wake document delivers without moving the watermark; a verified deletion mid-bulk stops the remaining documents (never verified, never re-chained) and skips the persist (as-is semantics, pinned); a wake deletion short-circuits before any bulk query; wake-then-bulk delivers appended-once + skipped-once with a single persist; a missing wake id is a no-op that still runs the bulk; no enrollment touches nothing; equal timestamps both handle and the strict `startAfter` excludes the tie on the next pass. Full JVM suite green after every campaign main-source change: 185 classes / 1,618 tests, 0 failures (2026-10-14).

**Honest limits.** (a) The fake models Firestore's query semantics; server-side nuances (multi-page >500-doc pull behavior, cache interaction) remain device/emulator-only — queued as Tier B/C in the campaign backlog. (b) The unsorted-page pin is defensive: real Firestore likely cannot return one, but the receiver's guards are now covered rather than trusted. (c) The lazy keystore changes first-touch timing only; base-class key methods still fail off-device by design — subclasses must override, which is the seam's contract.

## #149 — The Banner That Cried Wolf: Unresolved-Only Counting, Day-Split Re-Join, and a Resolved-Through Watermark (2026-10-15)

**Context.** Owner report: "Usage tracking was interrupted … 18 times in 18h 35 min … so some usage may not be counted" — re-appearing on a device where nothing was being lost, eroding confidence in the count itself. Device forensics (`ledger-outage` on the X6532) split the banner into one read-model defect over two legitimate mechanisms it was mis-reporting:

1. **Device sleep fabricates gap rows (RC-1).** The FGS is deliberately wakelock-free; when XOS suspends the CPU at screen-off, the first tick after wake measures the whole suspension as dead time and writes an honest `GAP_DETECTED` row. Probe: one controlled 185 s screen-off produced `measurement gap 206428ms …` (12:01:33, PID 9280) — nightly sleep alone manufactures a fresh row every morning.
2. **Real kills also produce rows (RC-2).** The 10-13 trace: an LMK SIGKILL (exit info) with 31 h without a restart (XOS ignores START_STICKY). This is the mechanism the banner was built for; it must keep reporting.
3. **The read model over-counted (RC-3).** `current()` counted every `GAP_DETECTED` row in the freshness window — including windows the platform had already answered (a recovery pass queries the window; whatever it held was credited, or nothing was held), including day-split siblings of one detection as separate interruptions, and including windows the user had already dismissed. Pre-fix `ledger-outage`: `count=18 largestWindowMs=66937886` — the 18×/18h35m banner reproduced exactly. Journal-vs-platform parity (Netflix) at the same window: diff 1,064 ms — nothing was actually being lost.

**Decision — five parts, one principle: the banner describes only windows the platform has not accounted for.**
1. `AccountingDiagnosticsState` gains `gapsResolvedThroughMs` — monotonic (`maxOf`), persisted with `commit()` per #146's apply()-loss doctrine (a lost flush here resurrects resolved windows) — and `promptEligibleGapMs` (the offer's own span, part 5).
2. `UsageDiagnosticsRecorder.recordRecovery`: every **numeric, non-bootBoundary** platform answer advances the watermark to that pass's wall clock, then either stores the offer's span (`markPromptEligible(now, gapSpanMs)`) on a prompt-eligible outcome or clears eligibility. A null/bootBoundary answer says nothing about the window and leaves the watermark untouched. Resolved ≠ deleted: the journal keeps every row (AD-15; day-audit and parity read them).
3. One-time upgrade seed: the first diagnostics pass with usage access calls `seedResolvedThrough(now)` — while the watermark is still 0, the pre-fix backlog is presumed resolved (with access, any gap could have been answered; the freshness window bounds what could ever have been reportable). Never seeds without usage access.
4. `AccountingOutageSource.current()` counts only unresolved windows (`row.wallClockTsMs > watermark`) and runs them through `AccountingDiagnostics.mergedOutageSpans` — day-split siblings (one detection written across local midnight = rows sharing `(wallClockTsMs, bootCount, source)` with contiguous elapsed bounds) re-join into one interruption before count/largest are computed. Separate detections never merge, even when abutting.
5. R8's exemption offer keeps its surface through resolution: when the stage is NONE (nothing unresolved), `offerReport` consults the offer's own evidence — `promptEligibleSinceMs` + `promptEligibleGapMs`, which travel with eligibility through every exit — and emits `OutageSummary(1, gapMs)` + the route it derives. Exempt devices, dismissed eligibilities, and consumed cooldowns yield nothing. #13's one-time "tracking can resume" confirmation is unchanged.

**Alternatives considered.** (a) *Suppress sleep gaps at detection time / wakelock the FGS* — a suspension and a kill are indistinguishable at detection time (both are "elapsed advanced, no ticks ran"); a wakelock in a 30 s-cadence FGS is a battery cost the product refuses, and it silences nothing about real kills. (b) *A second outage counter store* — AD-15: the ledger is the evidence; derived reads, not parallel stores. (c) *A size threshold ("ignore gaps below X")* — no size separates sleep from a short real outage, and naming either as *the* cause is exactly what AD-13 forbids. (d) *Delete or rewrite rows once resolved* — append-only journal + deterministic dedup; resolved windows are evidence (day-audit, parity), and "not reportable" belongs in the read model. (e) *Count only the newest window* — hides older windows that are genuinely still unaccounted; an unanswered window is an unanswered window.

**Verified.** JVM: 187 classes / 1,639 tests / 0 failures (2026-10-15; up from 185/1,618). New: `AccountingOutageSourceTest` (8, Robolectric full-stack read model over the real ledger), `AccountingDiagnosticsStateTest` (3), `UsageDiagnosticsRecorderTest` +5 (numeric resolves + stores the offer gap; null/bootBoundary leave the watermark; ineligible outcome resolves and drops the offer gap; seed fires only while 0 and never moves backward), `AccountingDiagnosticsTest` +5 (`mergedOutageSpans` incl. multi-day collapse, contiguous-but-separate detections, boot mismatch). Device (X6532, 2026-10-15): pre-fix `ledger-outage` `count=18 largestWindowMs=66,937,886` — the banner's own numbers; post-install `count=null promptRoute=null`; controlled 185 s screen-off → fresh `measurement gap 206428ms` landed in the journal and `count=null` still (the same tick's resolution marked it accounted); `ledger-day-audit` confirmed the row is kept (resolved ≠ deleted) and parity diff 1,064 ms; both probe rows purged (`deleted=1` each) restoring the device's clean journal.

**Honest limits.** (a) The watermark is wall-clock: a backward clock correction makes newly written rows read as already-resolved until the clock catches up, and a forward jump can step over a row a null answer left unresolved — both bounded by the 24 h freshness window and by same-tick resolution being the normal path (detected → answered in one pass). (b) The upgrade seed presumes the pre-fix backlog resolved — deliberate: those windows can no longer be queried, the freshness window bounds them, and a no-access device never seeds. (c) A device granted usage access later seeds at that first granted pass — a >24 h-old real outage from before the grant is not surfaced (same bound as (b)). (d) A long sleep window that was prompt-eligible can still raise the exemption offer on a non-exempt device — that offer's purpose is exactly eligible silence (cooldowns and dismissal apply); the report riding it is the offer's own evidence, never an attributed cause (AD-13).

## #150 — The Card That Said "Off" While It Was On: Fresh Consent Reads in the Rules Row (2026-10-15)

**Context.** Tier C stress (2026-10-15), VPN establish/revoke storm on the X6532: after a consent grant the Rules screen's "Website blocking is off" card stayed rendered while the tunnel was ACTIVE, and tapping that stale card silently did nothing. Device trail: the tunnel established at 12:30:13 (`starting website tunnel (live website rules present)` → `MARKER: vpn-state ACTIVE`, `tun0` up, traffic relaying), yet a 12:35 UI dump still read title "Website blocking is off" + `domain_usage_off_body`; a 12:38 tap on the card produced no dialog, no vpndialogs process/activity record, no app log line — while the appop's last-touch timestamp refreshed at the tap instant (the supported reading: the tap ran `VpnService.prepare()`, which returned null — consent on file — and `?.let{}` swallowed the result). Root cause: `engineConsented` was a `remember`ed snapshot refreshed only inside `LaunchedEffect(rules, engineStatus)`; neither key flips when consent lands or the tunnel establishes, so the flag went stale the moment a grant happened without a rule-set change. The card's tap branch mirrored that stale flag, took the "launch the consent dialog" path, and silently dead-ended. Real-user reachable: grant via the app's own dialog → return → tunnel up, card still "off" (`engineStatus` does not flip on establish — the stale 12:35 render proves it), re-tap dead.

**Decision — one principle: consent is read where it is used.**
1. `engineOffMessage` consults `VpnLifecycle.consented(context)` directly at composition. The screen's 5 s tick already recomposes it, so a grant or revoke self-heals the card within one tick; the `engineConsented` remember state is deleted (the effect keeps only its `ensure` duty).
2. The card's tap handler branches on `consentIntent()` itself, not on a mirrored flag: non-null → launch the system dialog; null (already granted) → `VpnLifecycle.ensure(...)` — the same decide()-routed retry every other start site uses, replacing the unconditional `start()`.

**Alternatives considered.** (a) *Refresh the remembered flag on a lifecycle observer* — fixes the text after dialog-return but leaves the tap stale in the granted-while-resumed window (the exact case this stress leg produced) and keeps a mirror that can drift. (b) *Key the effect on `VpnEngineState`* — no Flow exists for it (a `@Volatile` static); minting one to serve a mirror is more machinery than reading consent directly. (c) *Leave the tap as-is; the stale window is short* — it is not: the stall ends only at the next rules/status change or screen re-entry, and the failure mode is a dead tap on a card that explicitly says "Tap to turn it on".

**Verified.** Device (X6532, 2026-10-15, fix installed via `install -r`): the exact stale scenario (consent on file + tunnel ACTIVE + 2 live rules) now renders the healthy form — no card at all — against the captured pre-fix dump that showed the stale "off" card in the same state; with consent revoked and the tunnel down, the card correctly reappears with `domain_usage_off_body` and the tunnel stays down (`ensure` NONE on the fresh read), FGS foreground, rotation restored, 0 FATAL/ANR. Storm context (same leg): rule-churn ×3 via the real UI toggles (last-live-rule pause → STOP ≤200 ms; return → START ≈5.7 s; exactly 1:1 start/stop log pairs; N-1 rules → zero churn), kill-while-ACTIVE ×2 (framework tears the tun with the process; relaunch restores via `refreshWebsiteLimitTunnel`, cold ≈15–25 s), 12 taps, 0 crashes. No JVM test: the change is compose-side rendering plus a lambda route with no pure-logic unit — its decision logic is `decide()`'s existing exhaustive truth table.

**Honest limits.** (a) A user mid-grant can still see the off card for up to one 5 s tick before it clears. (b) An external consent flip with the screen composed and tunnel down self-heals on that same tick; a tap inside the window now routes to `ensure` and starts the tunnel correctly — verified by logic path and the post-fix device scenarios, not a live dialog re-grant under adb (the XOS dialog auto-decline makes that unscriptable). (c) An external appop flip does not tear a live tunnel on this ROM (appop is read at establish time only) — consent-revoked-while-up leaves the tunnel enforcing until the next stop/kill; platform behavior, documented, unchanged by this ADR.

## #151 — The Card That Told an In-App User to Open the App: Split the Paused Banner's Copy (2026-10-15)

**Context.** Tier B stress (2026-10-15, API-37 emulator), permission-revocation leg: revoking usage access yields `DEGRADED_NO_DETECTION`, whose Home banner rendered `protection_paused_text` — "MyExodus can't tell which app is open right now, so blocks won't start. **Open MyExodus** to see what's off and turn protection back on." The same resource is the degraded notification's body (`ProtectionNotifications.kt:73`), where "Open MyExodus" is exactly right: the notification lives outside the app and carries no button. The Home banner is inside the app and carries the Fix button directly below the sentence instructing the user to open the app. The strings.xml comment shows the sharing was deliberate ("the degraded notification and the Home banner share this copy", §8 Step 6), so the out-of-app instruction was authored for the notification and inherited by the card.

**Decision.** One string per surface, matched to each surface's affordances:
1. The notification keeps `protection_paused_text` — a buttonless surface must name the way in.
2. The Home banner gets `protection_paused_card_text`: "MyExodus can't tell which app is open right now, so blocks won't start. Tap Fix to turn protection back on." — it names the button it actually carries, so the instruction and the control below agree.
3. `protection_paused_title` and `protection_paused_action` stay shared — both surfaces render them, and neither is context-mismatched.

**Alternatives considered.** (a) *Share one neutral string that avoids "Open MyExodus"* — the notification's only path then goes unnamed, weakening the one surface that genuinely needs the instruction; splitting is cheaper than making both surfaces weaker. (b) *Drop the second sentence from the card entirely* — the problem statement then sits next to a bare, generically-named button ("Fix"); naming the button keeps what-to-do answered in text. (c) *Tell a card user to "open Protection settings"* — the app-internal route is the Fix hand-off the user sees; copy that names a destination they must find on their own is a step behind the button that is right there.

**Verified.** JVM: new `DegradationCopyTest` (2 tests, Robolectric reads against the real resources) — the card variant must not contain "Open MyExodus" and must name "Fix"; the notification variant must keep the instruction, so a silent re-merge of the strings fails the test. Targeted run green 2026-10-15 (2/2, 0 failures); full-suite run and device re-verification ride the Tier B fix batch (below in the same session's commits).

**Honest limits.** (a) The guard test pins these two resources; it does not prevent a future third in-app surface from reusing `protection_paused_text` — the class is caught for the known shape, not by pattern. (b) The fix is copy-only: no behavior change, and no device screenshot of the wrong sentence in its natural state was captured — the finding is source-checked (both call sites read) off the revocation leg that produced the card itself; the fix batch captures the corrected card on-screen.

## #152 — The Re-Arm That Never Fired: FGS Ensure on Every Process Start (2026-10-15)

**Context.** Owner report, recurring and "very annoying": the Home "Usage tracking was interrupted" banner (18× / 18h35m at its worst), the standing question "if accessibility is off why did screen for blocking later appear then", and — the same day — Instagram running unblocked through a live window. Forensics across the day's device sessions assembled the chain, every leg device-proven:
1. **XOS kills the FGS-hosting process.** 15:31:40 "prcp FGS" cgroup kill (X6532) — the third independently captured occurrence (previously: 2026-09-28, 17-min window; ADR #149's 31 h trace).
2. **START_STICKY is ignored on this ROM.** No restart ever arrives after the kill (all three occurrences); "sticky restart covers the kill case" was the false premise (now annotated at ADR #105's bullet).
3. **The old re-arm sites were app-open/toggle/boot/update only** (including ADR #107/#108's MY_PACKAGE_REPLACED), so between a kill and the next app open, enforcement stayed dead. The block screen "appearing later anyway" was Path B polling while the FGS process was alive — never the killed case — which is why the two observations looked contradictory.
4. **The ledger told the truth the whole time.** Each dead window became a GAP row; after ADR #149's read model, the banner counts only unresolved windows — so 18×/18h35m was this kill pattern accumulated, not a measurement bug.

**Decision — three parts, one principle: every process start re-arms the FGS.**
1. `EnforcementForegroundService.ensureOnProcessStart(context)` — reads `EnhancedModeSettings` itself (it runs before any service instance exists to carry the injected one), gates on `enhancedModeEnabled`, calls the shared `start()`. Called from `MyExodusApp.onCreate`'s post-unlock block (guarded by the existing `!underInstrumentation` gate).
2. No new waker was added. The always-present WorkManager cadence (HealthCheckWorker 15-min unique-name KEEP — re-enqueued by every post-unlock process start; the widget writer's 30-min backstop per ADR #145) already wakes a dead process — both paths device-proven — and that wake now re-arms the FGS. Worst-case dead window shrinks from "until the next app open" (18 h 35 m observed) to the ~15-min cadence class **for the kill classes the platform lets the cadence revive — Leg B below found that boundary and draws it**.
3. `start()` gains one containment: `ContextCompat.startForegroundService` throwing `IllegalStateException` (Android 12+ background-start refusal) is logged, not crashed. Legal on the X6532 (battery-exempt `user,com.example.myexodus,10414` + SYSTEM_ALERT_WINDOW); an OEM missing both degrades honestly — the D1 surfaces report the authority, the log names the refusal.
4. The FGS's existing exit-path repair (`checkForUserStop` → `repairAccountingGap` → ADR #149 watermark) resolves the kill window on revival — the crash-leg rows drop out of the banner via the same-tick resolution path.

**Alternatives considered.** (a) *A new AlarmManager/WorkManager watchdog* — ADR #105's rejection stands (force-stop is unwatchable; a dedicated loop is the smell the plan already ruled out). This ADR hooks a cadence that already exists rather than adding one. (b) *Continue relying on sticky restart* — device-falsified three times; no code path worth keeping. (c) *Always-on VPN* — still rejected (§8.4) and would not survive a killed process either. (d) *Mute the banner during known kills* — the banner is honest evidence; the fix is bounded revival, not a quieter report.

**Verified.** JVM: new `EnforcementFgsEnsureTest` (2 — gate on records the FGS start; gate off records nothing) and the full suite at **189 classes / 1,643 tests / 0 failures / 0 errors / 0 skipped** (2026-10-15). Device Leg A (X6532, wireless ADB; kill simulated with `am crash` while the process was backgrounded): dead at 16:29:12 (pid gone) → external wake 16:29:22 (ledger-read broadcast, **no app open**) → pid 15620 → FGS `isForeground=true` by 16:29:30 → tick 20 at 16:29:53 → `ledger-outage` 16:30:20 `count=null largestWindowMs=null` — the crash window was resolved by the revival, not accumulated (the direct answer to the popup complaint). **Device Leg B (kill → natural-cadence revival, hands-off, 18 min): NEGATIVE — and the negative found the sharper truth.** Second `am crash` 16:30:50; the process stayed dead 23 min 45 s. At 16:44-16:45 the package's due WorkManager jobs sat overdue (own constraints all satisfied — `job=true`) but **not ready**: `comp=false` (`IS_COMPONENT_USABLE` — plain `PackageManager.getServiceInfo` semantics per AOSP's `JobSchedulerService` dump), i.e. the platform considered the job services unusable **while the app had no process** — a dead-app chicken-and-egg: the cadence that was supposed to revive it is itself gated on the app being alive (a state `dumpsys package` reads as healthy: `stopped=false`, components `enabledComponents`). Forced runs were acknowledged (`Running job [FORCED]`) with no start and no logcat attempt. The revive came instead from the a11y bind: the planned grant-restore cycle (16:54:35) started the process within seconds (pid 21728), and the fix's re-arm fired automatically on that start — tick 20 at 16:54:40, `authority=FULL`, FGS `isForeground=true` — with the same jobs flipping `comp=true` while the app was alive. Interruption record across the whole leg: ticks 16:30:23 (old pid) → silence 23m45s → 16:54:40 (new pid, FGS back).
**What Leg B revises, stated plainly:** the "WM cadence is the keeper" premise holds for silent kills (ADR #145's day: a JobScheduler tick revived a dead process 8 min after a kill) but is **false for crash-record kills on this ROM** — the 2026-10-15 `am crash` class left the job subsystem refusing to deliver to the dead app (`comp=false`), which matches the shape of the owner's 18 h 35 m windows (deaths the platform would not let the cadence revive). The fix remains exactly right for its job — *every* revival now restores enforcement (Leg A and Leg B's a11y revival both prove the arm; pre-fix revivals like 10-14 left the FGS down) — but revival itself is not guaranteed app-side. The device's strongest posture is therefore a11y ON: the a11y bind is a start path the platform honors for a dead app (device-proven 16:54:35), so the standing degraded-polling-only state (a11y off) removes the only revival net the crash class leaves. Whether the live XOS "prcp FGS" kill (cgroup SIGKILL, silent class) sits on the revivable or blocked side of this split remains unverified — recorded, not assumed.

**Honest limits.** (a) The kill legs simulate the XOS kill with `am crash` (SIGKILL-class signal); the LMK/"prcp FGS" kill itself was captured live at 15:31:40 today, and the revival path is signal-source-independent. (b) A user force-stop remains by-design dead (platform unwatchable — ADR #105's rejection is about that case and still holds). (c) Worst-case windows are class-dependent, and Leg B drew the line: silent kills sit in the WM cadence class (15–30 min, doze-deferred — ADR #145's 8-min JobScheduler revival), while crash-record kills on the X6532 get **no app-side revival at all** (`comp=false` blocks every due job while the app is dead; Leg B's 23 m 45 s dead window, ended only by an a11y bind). The change bounds the window wherever a waker exists; it never invents one — and the a11y binding is, empirically, the one platform start path a dead (even crash-killed) app still receives. (d) **Widget liveness finding (recorded):** at 15:33:08 the widget rendered PROTECTED (`carried=true, checkedAgo=null`) while enforcement was actually down — `toEnforcementStatus` maps authority, and authority ≠ liveness; the process-start ensure makes that mapping honest-by-construction in the dominant case, but a kill between widget writes still ages stale until the next scheduled write (ADR #145's UNVERIFIED transition is the designed backstop). (e) On OEMs where the background FGS start is refused, revival degrades to pre-fix behavior plus a log line — stated, not hidden; the verification device permits it.

## #153 — The 20-Second Cold Start: Raw-Key SQLCipher (2026-10-15)

**Context.** Owner report (task #12): cold start on the X6532 takes "way too long" — and worse than impatience, it is an enforcement gap: within one observed session the owner could open Instagram freely, and only once MyExodus finished loading did Instagram start getting blocked. Baseline capture (`/tmp/fixA-cold.log`, 18:01): Displayed +4 s 195 ms, **Fully drawn +19 s 915 ms**, and five driver-opens of `myexodus_rules.db` in a single cold start — 18:01:11.005, then :16.735, :20.342, :23.407, :26.354 — each logging `dek=254ms key=5719ms`. Root cause, measured not guessed: SQLCipher's PBKDF2 key derivation runs per connection and cost ~5.7 s per keying on this SoC; five keyings ≈ 20 s bound time-to-first-frame. The KDF is pure overhead here — its job is turning a low-entropy passphrase into a key, while our DEK is *already* a random 256-bit key (Keystore-wrapped), so PBKDF2 protected nothing that wasn't already protected. The five keyings come from the zetetic native handle's internal connection pool: Room itself opens exactly one driver per process (the earlier Fix A hypothesis — a Room-level single-connection-pool flag — was device-measured **null**, attribution falsified; the flag stays defensively, comment corrected).

**Decision — raw-key mode; keying state in the file; one-time self-healing migration.**
1. `SqlCipherRawKey.passphraseFor(dek)` produces SQLCipher's hex-literal form: 67 bytes of ASCII `x'<64 lowercase hex>'`. The codec detects the `x'` prefix and uses the 64 hex digits as the raw AES key — PBKDF2 is skipped entirely.
2. `ensureRawKeyed(path, dek, raw)` is the single entry at every open site: probe with the raw passphrase first; a legacy-keyed file fails that open on the header read and **cannot be modified by it**, and only then does `rekeyLegacyToRaw` run — `PRAGMA wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE)` (flush so the rekey rewrites only the main file), `PRAGMA rekey = "x'...'"`, then verify the raw open + read before returning. A crash mid-rekey rolls back to the legacy keying; the next launch re-probes and retries. No flag exists anywhere — keying state lives in the file, so the migration self-heals by construction.
3. A missing/fresh file is created raw-keyed by the probe itself — first-run databases never see PBKDF2.
4. Wired at every open site: `KeyFetchingSQLCipherDriver` (every Room route to the rules store), `ActivityLogStore.open`/`reopenFresh`, and `DatabaseMigration`'s activity-log destination (born raw). The pre-split legacy file inside `DatabaseMigration` deliberately stays legacy-keyed — it predates this ADR, its key material is the old key, and it is read once.
5. The passphrase array must outlive the open (zetetic re-reads it when the native pool opens further connections): ownership is explicit — `ZeroingConnection` and ActivityLogStore's `rawKey` field hold it, each zeroed at close.

**The trap the device caught — `execSQL` runs the rekey, then throws.** The first implementation used `legacy.execSQL("PRAGMA rekey = ...")`. On the X6532 this threw `android.database.sqlite.SQLiteException: unknown error (code 0): Queries can be performed using SQLiteDatabase query or rawQuery methods only` from `nativeExecuteForChangedRowCount` — **after the rekey had already executed natively**. PRAGMA rekey emits a result row; zetetic's execSQL executes the statement and then rejects its shape. Observed effect (18:24 capture): rekey done, verification skipped, the failure propagated ("Rules DB open failed with SQLiteException" → factory returned null via the health path), and a retry ~30 ms later succeeded because the file was already raw — the migration worked only by the luck of a retry. Fixed to `legacy.rawExecSQL(...)` (javap-verified: `public void rawExecSQL(java.lang.String, java.lang.Object...)` accepts result-row statements).

**Alternatives considered.** (a) *Room-level single connection pool (Fix A)* — device-measured null; keying happens below Room in the native handle (Context); kept defensively. (b) *Reuse one open connection across processes* — impossible (separate processes) and it would not remove the KDF anyway. (c) *`PRAGMA kdf_iter=1`* — still pays a KDF pass per open and mutates a security parameter away from SQLCipher defaults post-hoc; raw key skips the KDF step instead of weakening it. (d) *Plain SQLite store* — a real security regression, rejected on sight.

**Verified.** X6532, 2026-10-15, three captures:
- *Pre-fix baseline*: five `open myexodus_rules.db` lines, `key=5719ms` each; Displayed +4 s 195 ms; Fully drawn +19 s 915 ms.
- *Migration (fixed code, live capture `/tmp/fixB-rekey-proof2.log`)*: 18:31:17.458 the probe rejects the legacy file (code 26 → `isWrongKey` classifies) → 18:31:21.418 `rekeyed myexodus_rules.db legacy→raw; PBKDF2 retired (one-time, ADR #153)` → `open … dek=33ms probe=3996ms key=4ms rekeyed=legacy→raw` (the ~4 s probe includes the one-time legacy PBKDF2) → 18:31:21.481 snapshot v44 "2 app, 2 url rules". No "open failed" anywhere — the rawExecSQL fix verified end-to-end on the device. Bonus property, observed: the migration fired inside the process started by `install -r` (the MY_PACKAGE_REPLACED receiver path that re-arms the FGS per ADR #107/#108), so a real user's first manual open after an update is already raw-keyed; either entry point self-heals regardless.
- *Steady state (fixed code, cold start post-migration, `/tmp/fixB-steady-final.log`)*: `open myexodus_rules.db dek=46ms probe=40ms key=5ms` (no rekey flag), am start -W TotalTime 2937 ms, Displayed +2 s 937 ms, **Fully drawn +4 s 320 ms** (vs +19 s 915 ms), second process opens at 16 ms; app-init laps: super +296 ms → post-unlock init done t+384 ms; snapshot v44 "2 app, 2 url rules" both after the migration and after the steady run — data intact.
- *Scale (stress leg, `/tmp/fixB-stress4000.log`)*: the product's own designed maximum — 2000 + 2000 rules (the repo-capped ceiling), seeded directly via DAO — leaves the keying path flat (`dek=60ms probe=45ms key=9ms` vs 46/40/5 at 2 rules) and first paint near-flat (Displayed +3 s 487, Fully drawn +5 s 441 vs +4 s 320 at 2 rules, within/at the edge of this device's ±0.5–2 s run variance); the 4004-entry authoritative snapshot rebuild runs in the background and published *after* fully-drawn without blocking it (fast-path cache served the first frame). **The same leg surfaced an unrelated UI-scale defect, queued as its own task:** a later MainActivity session with the 4004 rules ANR'd (input-dispatch, "Waited 5000ms for MotionEvent", `/data/anr/anr_2026-10-15-18-42-36-647`; main thread idle in the dump → a transient main-thread stall, not this fix's path). Stress data fully removed post-leg (cleanup verified: `removed=4000`, baseline 4 intact); repro = one seed command (`StartupScaleDeviceTest#seedStressRules`).
- JVM: new `SqlCipherRawKeyTest` (4) pins the 67-byte format — which caught the author's own 68-vs-67 off-by-one before any device run.

**Honest limits.** (a) Raw-key mode removes the KDF-per-open; it changes nothing about what protects the file at rest — AES-256 under the same Keystore-wrapped DEK, and possession of the DEK bytes is equivalent to before, minus the PBKDF2 cost an attacker with the same random key would also have skipped. (b) The remaining ~4.3 s fully-drawn is now other work (process fork ~0.3 s, app init 0.38 s, activity/Hilt class path ~1.1 s, compose first frame, FGS/VPN establish) — PBKDF2 no longer appears on the profile; further wins belong to a different change (e.g. a baseline profile), not this one. (c) The one-time migration cost is real: the first launch after the update pays one legacy PBKDF2 (~4 s here) plus the rekey — usually absorbed by the package-replace receiver run before the user opens the app (observed above); any interruption self-heals with no data risk (the probe cannot modify a file it cannot key). (d) An app *downgrade* to a pre-#153 build cannot open a rekeyed file (PBKDF2 keying reads a raw-keyed header as wrong-key) — accepted: direct-APK distribution only, no downgrade path (no Play; OPD). (e) The probe-by-open adds one extra open of the file per process (≈40 ms steady) — measured, bounded, and the price of flagless self-healing. (f) Devices that already ran the buggy execSQL path (this X6532, during verification) are raw-keyed with no "rekeyed" log line for that transition — the state is correct (verified by subsequent opens) but the one-time evidence exists only for the fixed-path capture above.

## #154 — The Disk-Full Crash Loop: Firestore Memory Cache + WM Task-Executor Guard (2026-10-15)

**Context.** Tier B stress (B7 leg, 2026-10-15): with `/data` at ~16 MB free, the app entered a respawn-and-die loop — two independent throwables, one class of outcome.
1. **Firestore's on-disk SQLite** (the SDK's default persistence layer) threw internal RuntimeExceptions on the main thread under ENOSPC — "cannot rollback - no transaction is active" — killing the process on each attempt.
2. **WorkManager 2.11.2's startup `ForceStopRunnable`** threw `IllegalStateException` (wrapping the SQLite ENOSPC failure) on every process respawn, every ~7 s — each respawn died before the a11y service could re-bind, and the platform moved the a11y service into the crashed set: a dead app with blocking down, on a device whose whole job is blocking.
Both throwables reach a thread default handler because both run on threads the SDK owns — WM's internal tasks run on `Configuration.taskExecutor`, and anything that escapes there has no app-side catch point by construction.

**Decision — prevention for both throwers, containment for the executor class.**
1. **Firestore memory-only cache.** `provideFirebaseFirestore()` installs `FirestoreSettings` with `MemoryCacheSettings` — no on-disk Firestore SQLite exists by construction, so ENOSPC inside it cannot occur. Durable offline state is the app's own Room stores (rules, ledger, accountability); every Firestore read path either waits for server snapshots (`PartnerLiveSync` skips `isFromCache`) or is guarded and retried next tick (mailbox pull), so nothing depended on the SDK's disk cache for correctness.
2. **`GuardedTaskExecutor`.** `MyExodusApp.workManagerConfiguration` wraps WM's task executor: `Exception` from any WM internal task is logged and contained (the task aborts; the process lives); `Error` (OOM etc.) still propagates to the default handler. One wrapper covers every present and future WM-internal thrower — the ForceStopRunnable case is contained even if a future WM version throws something new at full disk.
3. **The two compose.** Prevention removes the known throwers; containment bounds the unknown ones. Deliberately no third mechanism (no retry loop, no disk-space preflight): with the disk full, the honest contract is *the app stays alive and the next process start with space re-runs the same startup work*.

**Alternatives considered.** (a) *Keep Firestore persistence, catch the ENOSPC path* — the throwable is raised inside SDK internals ("no transaction is active") and is not reliably attributable to ENOSPC at the catch site; removing the disk consumer is smaller than classifying inside the SDK. (b) *`setTaskExecutor` with a try/catch around only ForceStopRunnable* — that specific class would still crash the executor thread on the next WM version's new failure; the wrapper is one line more and covers the class. (c) *Catch `Throwable` in the guard* — swallows OOM into a zombie process; `Exception`-only is the deliberate line. (d) *WM on-demand initialization (`androidx.startup` removal + lazy init)* — adds a configuration surface and delays jobs; provides no containment, which is the actual gap.

**Verified.** Emulator leg (API-37 AVD, 2026-10-15 evening; the sanctioned Tier B device). Preparation: built APK (contains `GuardedTaskExecutor` — dex-verified), `install -r`, AOT `cmd package compile -m speed -f` (B-01 mandate for this 1-vCPU VM; without it every cold start ANRs).
- *Pre-fix baseline* (B7, same session): the respawn-and-die loop above, ~7 s per respawn, driving the disk fill.
- *Post-fix run 1 (`/tmp/b10-diskfull-post.log`, 28 MB free, cold start)*: process survived; `WM-ForceStopRunnable` ran cleanup and rescheduled normally; all later WM debug flow normal; **0 FATAL, 0 am_crash, 0 died lines**; the run doubled as a live demonstration of ADR #153 self-healing under load — the emulator's legacy-keyed `myexodus_rules.db` was probed (17.5 s: ENOSPC makes PBKDF2 crawl), classified wrong-key, rekeyed (`rekeyed myexodus_rules.db legacy→raw`), and the snapshot published — migration correct under disk pressure, no data touched.
- *Post-fix run 2 (`/tmp/b10-disc16-fresh.log`, 16 MB free — the pre-fix crash level — with `androidx.work.workdb` deleted first so WM must create its database from scratch)*: ForceStopRunnable cleanup + reschedule completed; workdb recreated; 0 FATAL.
- *Post-fix run 3 (`/tmp/b10-disc2mb.log`, ~2 MB free — harsher than the pre-fix scene — workdb deleted again)*: workdb recreated, **all three WM workers ran to completion** (`HealthCheckWorker` SUCCESS, `PartnerSyncWorker` SUCCESS, reschedule flow normal), pid stable, 0 FATAL; summary `/tmp/b10-evidence-summary.txt`. **This is the affirmative containment proof: at disk pressure where the pre-fix build could not complete one WM startup, the fixed build finished every scheduled job.**
- *Recovery*: fill removed bottom-up (mis-placed first fill on the root FS discovered via `df` and removed), emulator back to 4.6 GB free; clean cold start renders Home normally (`/tmp/b10-emulator-recovered.png`).
- JVM: `GuardedTaskExecutorTest` (3 — exception contained and following tasks still run; every command reaches the delegate; `Error` not swallowed) + `FirestoreCacheSettingsTest` (1 — the provider's settings carry a memory-cache instance).
- Commit note: the WM wiring shipped in `e8d2cf1` referencing `GuardedTaskExecutor` before its file was tracked (HEAD did not compile standalone); repaired in `a399174` alongside this ADR's code — stated plainly because a bisect landing on `e8d2cf1` will fail to build.

**Honest limits.** (a) The guard never fired in the emulator runs — no exception escaped to contain at the tested pressures (the fixes prevented the throwers before the catch point). The catch path itself is pinned by unit test only; device-level evidence for the *prevention* is the three surviving runs, not a captured "WorkManager task aborted" line. Stated because "survived disk-full" and "guard engaged" are different proofs. (b) The emulator is the sanctioned exception device (ruling: physical X6532 first; AVD for XOS-neutral stress legs) — these legs are disk-pressure semantics, not XOS behavior. (c) ENOSPC below the tested ~2 MB free on userdata was not probed; a future failure mode in that sliver would be an unknown, not a regression of this ADR. (d) The emulator's a11y state after the legs shows the standard force-stop artifact ("Blocker off" banner) — expected, not a defect; it clears on the app's own re-enable tap, which was not injected (unchanged policy: never inject taps on a device the owner may be using). (e) Memory-only Firestore cache means a cold app with no network sees no cached partner documents — the app surfaces last-known Room state instead; accepted (design already leaned on server snapshots for correctness).
